Landing Page Code Walkthrough

Every file behind the new landing page on the new-landing-page branch: what it does, how the files connect, and the important parts of the real source with plain English notes. 81 files, about 14,039 lines. Code excerpts are copied from the actual files; line numbers refer to the files as they were when this was written (October 2026).

How to use this

  1. If you are new, read the Before you start list and keep the Glossary open.
  2. Follow the reading order below. Each section card lists its files, the order to read them in, annotated excerpts, and one or more flows (step by step traces of what happens at runtime).
  3. Open the real file side by side in your editor. The excerpts show the parts that matter; the editor shows everything around them.
  4. Use the file index to answer "where is this used?" for any file.

Reading order

  1. Foundations first: page wiring, tokens and mixins. Everything else depends on them.
  2. Static sections: DomainIdentityOS and IdentityOsProvidersStats. Layout and styling with almost no JS.
  3. Scroll reveals: DomainRoadmap, then DomainAdoptionGap. The IntersectionObserver plus data attribute pattern.
  4. The hero: IdentityOsHero and Input. Several small animated pieces and the search flow.
  5. Charts and state driven motion: DomainGrowthComparison, DomainLivingIdentity, HowItWorksJourney.
  6. Counters and loops: DomainOnchainStats, IdentityOsStatsAndPartners.
  7. Hardest last: OwnershipComparison and OnchainIdentityNetwork, the scroll pinned stories.
  8. Data and polish: DomainBlogTeaser and the FAQ.

Page wiring and foundations

Before any landing page section can appear, Next.js needs an HTML shell, a root component that loads global CSS and wraps every page in shared providers, and the page file itself, which fetches blog posts at build time and stacks the sections in order. This part covers that plumbing, plus the design tokens and breakpoint mixins every section stylesheet leans on, and the small data layer that turns Ghost CMS posts into the cards in the blog teaser.

FileLinesRole
pages/_document.tsx74The outer HTML shell: lang attribute, font preconnects and async font stylesheets.
pages/_app.tsx185Root component for every page: global CSS imports and the provider tree.
pages/index.tsx209The landing page: SEO meta, page state, section order and getStaticProps.
pages/index.module.scss33Page wrapper, section column and the floating scroll to top button.
src/design-system/composites/landing-page/index.ts13Barrel file that reexports every landing page section from one path.
src/design-system/layouts/mainLayout/index.tsx298Shared layout: head tags, third party scripts, Header, main and Footer.
src/design-system/styles/main.scss5Global stylesheet entry that pulls in fonts and tokens.
src/design-system/styles/tokens.scss433CSS custom properties (colors, type, spacing, layout) plus responsive overrides.
src/design-system/styles/_mixins.scss156Sass breakpoint mixins and small utility mixins.
src/lib/ghost.ts229Server only Ghost CMS client and the adapter that shapes posts into BlogSummary.
src/lib/views.ts62Fetches view counts for blog posts from the Endless API.
src/lib/blog-utils.ts35Pagination helper and the formatDate used by blog cards.
src/data/blogs.ts19The BlogSummary type shared by every blog component.

Read in this order

  1. pages/index.tsx: it is the page itself, and everything else in this section exists to serve it.
  2. src/design-system/composites/landing-page/index.ts: shows where all those section imports come from.
  3. src/data/blogs.ts: the one data shape the page passes down, tiny but referenced everywhere.
  4. src/lib/ghost.ts and src/lib/views.ts: how getStaticProps fills that shape.
  5. src/lib/blog-utils.ts: the date formatter the blog card calls.
  6. src/design-system/layouts/mainLayout/index.tsx: what wraps the sections (head tags, Header, Footer).
  7. pages/_app.tsx and pages/_document.tsx: the outermost layers Next.js wraps around every page.
  8. src/design-system/styles/main.scss, tokens.scss, _mixins.scss: the styling vocabulary every section stylesheet uses.
  9. pages/index.module.scss: last, because it is short and only makes sense once you know what it wraps.

pages/_document.tsx 74 lines

In the Next.js pages router, _document.tsx renders only on the server and describes the static outer HTML (the <html>, <head> and <body> tags) that every page is poured into. React never runs it in the browser, so it holds things that never change between pages, like font links.

Lines 1–21
import { Html, Head, Main, NextScript } from 'next/document'
import Document from 'next/document'

export default class MyDocument extends Document {
  render() {
    return (
      <Html lang="en">
        <Head>
// …
          <link rel="preconnect" href="https://api.fontshare.com" crossOrigin="" />
          <link rel="preconnect" href="https://fonts.googleapis.com" crossOrigin="" />
          <link rel="preconnect" href="https://fonts.gstatic.com" crossOrigin="" />

Html, Head, Main and NextScript are special Next.js components: Main is where your page markup goes and NextScript is where the JavaScript bundles are injected. A preconnect link tells the browser to open a network connection (DNS lookup plus TLS handshake) to a domain early, so the later font request does not pay that cost on the critical path.

Lines 30–37
          <link
            rel="stylesheet"
            href="https://api.fontshare.com/v2/css?f[]=satoshi@400,500,700&display=swap"
            crossOrigin=""
            media="print"
            // @ts-ignore — plain HTML `onload` attribute, not React's onLoad
            onload="this.media='all'"
          />

This is the async stylesheet trick. A stylesheet with media="print" does not block rendering on screen, so the browser downloads it in the background. When it finishes, the inline onload attribute flips media to all and the font applies. The comment above it explains why it is a raw HTML attribute: React's own onLoad prop would be stripped from server output, and // @ts-ignore silences TypeScript, which does not know about a lowercase onload prop.

The result is that text first paints in a fallback font and swaps to Satoshi once it arrives (display=swap in the URL asks for exactly that).

Lines 54–74
          <noscript>
            <link
              rel="stylesheet"
              href="https://api.fontshare.com/v2/css?f[]=satoshi@400,500,700&display=swap"
              crossOrigin=""
            />
            <link
              rel="stylesheet"
              href="https://fonts.googleapis.com/css2?family=Space+Grotesk:wght@400;500;700&family=Plus+Jakarta+Sans:wght@400;800&family=DM+Sans:wght@400;500;700&display=swap"
              crossOrigin=""
            />
          </noscript>
        </Head>
        <body>
          <Main />
          <NextScript />
        </body>
      </Html>
    )
  }
}

The <noscript> copies load the same fonts normally for visitors with JavaScript turned off, since the onload swap would never fire for them. The body is just the two Next.js placeholders.

pages/_app.tsx 185 lines

_app.tsx is the root component Next.js renders around every page. Whatever page the visitor opens arrives here as Component, with its data in pageProps. For the landing page two things matter: the global CSS it imports, and the stack of context providers that hooks like useDispatch depend on. Wallet, session and login logic in this file is shared site wide and is only skimmed here.

Lines 34–56
// Import styles
import 'bootstrap/dist/css/bootstrap.min.css';
import 'react-toastify/dist/ReactToastify.css';
import '@fortawesome/fontawesome-svg-core/styles.css';
import 'react-loading-skeleton/dist/skeleton.css';
import styles from '@/design-system/primitives/toast-message/Toast.module.scss';
import './translate/google.css';
// @solana/wallet-adapter-react-ui/styles.css removed from global import — it carried
// a render-blocking @import for DM Sans via Google Fonts on the critical path.
// The wallet picker UI is handled by AppKit; if the Solana adapter modal is ever
// needed again, import this CSS inside the specific component that uses it.

Lines 69–98
export default function App({ Component, pageProps }: AppProps) {
  const [queryClient] = React.useState(() => new QueryClient());
  const [endpoint, setEndpoint] = useState<string | null>(null);
  const [wallets, setWallets] = useState<WalletAdapter[]>([]);
  const ROUTER = useRouter();
// …
  useEffect(() => {
    setEndpoint(MAINNET_RPC);
    // Dynamic import keeps the Phantom / Solflare adapter code out of the
    // initial bundle. They load asynchronously after mount, leaving the
    // critical path free and cutting the 431 ms blocking task on mobile.
    import('@solana/wallet-adapter-wallets').then(
      ({ PhantomWalletAdapter, SolflareWalletAdapter }) => {
        setWallets([new PhantomWalletAdapter(), new SolflareWalletAdapter()])
      }
    )
  }, []);
// …
  useEffect(() => {
    createAppKit({
      adapters: [wagmiAdapter, solanaAdapter],
      networks: [...APPKIT_NETWORKS],
      projectId: projectId(),
      features: { analytics: true },
    })
  }, []);

useState(() => new QueryClient()) uses a lazy initializer so the client is created once, not on every render. The two effects run only in the browser after the first paint: one loads the Solana wallet adapters with a dynamic import() (which splits them into a separate JavaScript file fetched later), and one sets up the AppKit wallet modal. The comments explain that both used to slow the landing page's largest paint when they ran earlier.

Lines 119–130
  // Deferred session check — runs after the browser is idle so the
  // redirectauth/session request (previously ~5.1s on the critical chain)
  // no longer competes with the LCP image fetch.
  useEffect(() => {
    if (typeof window === 'undefined') return
    if ('requestIdleCallback' in window) {
      const id = (window as any).requestIdleCallback(handleCheckSession, { timeout: 3000 })
      return () => (window as any).cancelIdleCallback(id)
    }
    const id = setTimeout(handleCheckSession, 0)
    return () => clearTimeout(id)
  }, []);

requestIdleCallback asks the browser to run a function when it has nothing more urgent to do, here with a 3 second upper limit. The session check therefore waits until the page has settled. Browsers without that API (Safari) use the setTimeout(..., 0) fallback. Each branch returns a cleanup function that cancels the pending call if the component unmounts.

Lines 132–152
  return (
    <GoogleOAuthProvider clientId={clientId}>
      <WagmiProvider config={wagmiAdapter.wagmiConfig}>
        <QueryClientProvider client={queryClient}>
          <Provider store={store}>
            <InitProvider>
              <AppContent
                Component={Component}
                pageProps={pageProps}
                endpoint={endpoint}
                wallets={wallets}
              />
              <AutoLogoutHandler />
              <SessionSyncHandler />
            </InitProvider>
          </Provider>
        </QueryClientProvider>
      </WagmiProvider>
    </GoogleOAuthProvider>
  );
}

This is the provider tree. A provider is a component that puts a value into React context, so any descendant can read it with a hook instead of receiving it as a prop. From outside in: Google login, Wagmi (Ethereum wallets), React Query (request caching), Redux (the app store; pages/index.tsx calls useDispatch and MainLayout calls useSelector, so both need it), and InitProvider from pages/Initial.tsx.

Order matters because a hook can only see providers above it, which is why Redux sits outside AppContent, where the page renders.

  }

  const [newSubscriber, setNewSubscriber] = useState(false)
  const [querySettled, setQuerySettled] = useState(false)
  const [showScrollTop, setShowScrollTop] = useState(false)
  const heroRef = useRef<HTMLDivElement>(null)

  const router = useRouter()
  const dispatch = useDispatch()

meta is a plain object of SEO fields handed to MainLayout, which turns it into <title> and <meta> tags. BASE_URL keeps the canonical link absolute.

The three state values are newSubscriber (did the URL say ?subscriber=new), querySettled (has a two second grace period passed) and showScrollTop. heroRef is a ref: a box whose .current holds the real DOM element after render, used here to measure the hero's height.

Lines 109–143
  useEffect(() => {
    const handleScroll = () => {
      const heroHeight = heroRef.current?.offsetHeight ?? 0
      setShowScrollTop(window.scrollY > heroHeight)
    }
    window.addEventListener('scroll', handleScroll, { passive: true })
    return () => window.removeEventListener('scroll', handleScroll)
  }, [])

  const scrollToTop = () => {
    window.scrollTo({ top: 0, behavior: 'smooth' })
  }

  useEffect(() => {
    return () => {
      dispatch(resetEventPop())
      dispatch(resetSubscriberPop())
    }
  }, [dispatch])

  useEffect(() => {
    const { query } = router

    if (query.subscriber === 'new') {
      setNewSubscriber(true)
    }
    const timeOut = setTimeout(() => {
      setQuerySettled(true)
    }, 2000)

    return () => {
      clearTimeout(timeOut)
      setQuerySettled(false)
    }
  }, [router])

The first effect adds a scroll listener once (empty dependency array) and removes it on unmount. It compares window.scrollY with the hero's height, so the scroll to top button only appears after you scroll past the hero. { passive: true } promises the browser the handler never cancels scrolling, which keeps scrolling smooth.

The second effect only has a cleanup: leaving the page resets two popup flags in Redux. The third reads the router query, opens the subscribe modal if it asks for it, and only after 2 seconds sets querySettled, so the modal never flashes during page load.

Lines 184–209
export const getStaticProps: GetStaticProps<HomePageProps> = async () => {
  if (!process.env.NEXT_GHOST_API_URL || !process.env.NEXT_GHOST_CONTENT_API_KEY) {
    console.info('[home] No Ghost credentials configured — using local-dev placeholder posts.')
    return {
      props: { blogPosts: DEV_FALLBACK_POSTS },
      revalidate: 30,
    }
  }

  try {
    const rawFeatured = await getFeaturedPosts()
    const views = await getBatchViews(rawFeatured.map(p => p.slug))
    const blogPosts = rawFeatured.map(p => toSummary(p, views[p.slug] ?? 0))

    return {
      props: { blogPosts },
      revalidate: 60,
    }
  } catch (err) {
    console.error('[home] Ghost fetch failed:', err)
    return {
      props: { blogPosts: [] },
      revalidate: 30,
    }
  }
}

getStaticProps runs at build time on the server, never in the browser. It returns the props for Home plus revalidate, which turns on Incremental Static Regeneration (ISR): Next.js may rebuild this page in the background at most once per that many seconds.

There are three outcomes. No credentials gives the placeholders and a 30 second window. Success fetches up to five featured posts, looks up all their view counts in one batch request, converts each with toSummary, and revalidates every 60 seconds. Any error is logged and an empty list is returned for 30 seconds, which makes DomainBlogTeaser render nothing. Because the error is caught, a brief Ghost outage replaces the cached page with one that has no blog section until the next successful rebuild.

pages/index.module.scss 33 lines

The page level CSS module. It is small on purpose: each section styles itself, so this file only frames them.

Lines 1–19
.pageWrapper {
  position: relative;
  width: 100%;
  min-height: 100vh;
  // Deliberately not overflow: hidden — several ported identity-os sections
  // (OwnershipComparison, OnchainIdentityNetwork) rely on position: sticky for their
  // scroll-pinned reveal, and ANY ancestor with overflow other than visible breaks
  // that: the sticky element then tries to pin against this wrapper's own scroll
  // position instead of the page's, and since this wrapper itself never scrolls, the
  // section just scrolls normally instead of pinning. Those sections already clip
  // their own decorative overflow internally, so a page-level clip isn't needed here.
}

// Matches the identity-os page's own .main — a plain flex column stacking the
// ported sections in order.
.main {
  display: flex;
  flex-direction: column;
}

.pageWrapper is deliberately left without overflow: hidden. The comment explains a rule beginners often trip over: position: sticky pins an element relative to its nearest scrolling ancestor, and any ancestor with overflow set to something other than visible becomes that ancestor. Clipping here would silently break the scroll pinned sections further down, and the FAQ's sticky left column too.

.main is a flex column, so sections stack in source order.

The layout shared by most pages of the site.   'Search, register, and manage your Web3 domain across ENS, Unstoppable Domains, and more — one-time payment, minted directly to your wallet with Endless Domains.'

// Guards every SEO tag against undefined/null/''/' ' from a page or an upstream API —
// a blank string is still truthy-looking in JSX but renders as content="", so trim first.
const safeTrim = (value?: string | null): string => (value ?? '').trim()

// Freshworks widget script — account/widget ID configurable via env so the embed
// can point at a different Freshworks instance per environment without a code change.
const FRESHWORKS_ACCOUNT_ID = process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_FRESHWORKS_ACCOUNT_ID || '32581950'
const FRESHWORKS_WIDGET_ID = process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_FRESHWORKS_WIDGET_ID || '1433239'

const MainLayout = ({ metaInfo, children, transparentHeader, noHeader, noFooter, alwaysStickyHeader, hideHeaderActions, externalizeNav }: LayoutType) => {

  const cartState = useSelector((state: RootState) => state.cart)
  const dispatch = useDispatch()
  const pathname = usePathname()
  const router = useRouter()
  const canonical = metaInfo.canonicalUrl ?? `${BASE_URL}${pathname ?? '/'}`
  const influencerKey = (pathname ? pathname.startsWith('/') : "") ? pathname.slice(1) : pathname

  // Resolved, never-empty SEO fields — OG/Twitter variants inherit from title/description
  // unless a page explicitly overrides them.
  const title = safeTrim(metaInfo.title) || DEFAULT_TITLE
  const description = safeTrim(metaInfo.description) || DEFAULT_DESCRIPTION
  const ogTitle = safeTrim(metaInfo.ogTitle) || title
  const ogDescription = safeTrim(metaInfo.ogDescription) || description
  const ogImage = metaInfo.ogImage || DEFAULT_OG_IMAGE
  const twitterTitle = safeTrim(metaInfo.twitterTitle) || title
  const twitterDescription = safeTrim(metaInfo.twitterDescription) || description
  const twitterImage = metaInfo.twitterImage || ogImage

safeTrim turns undefined, null or a whitespace only string into an empty string, and the || fallbacks then pick a default. So every SEO tag always has real content, and the Open Graph and Twitter variants inherit the page title and description unless the page overrides them. The landing page sets canonicalUrl explicitly, so line 44 uses it instead of building one from pathname.

src/design-system/styles/main.scss 5 lines

The global stylesheet entry point, imported once by pages/_app.tsx. It is two imports long.

Lines 1–5
 // 1. Font imports (must come before everything)
 @import './fonts.scss';

 // 4. Your CSS custom properties (available to all *.module.scss)
 @import './tokens.scss';

fonts.scss contains only a comment explaining that fonts are loaded from _document.tsx, so it adds no CSS. tokens.scss is the real payload: it defines the custom properties on :root. Because this file is imported globally, those properties exist on every page, which is what lets each *.module.scss file use var(--space-4) without importing anything.

src/design-system/styles/tokens.scss 433 lines

Design tokens: named values for colors, type, spacing and layout, written as CSS custom properties (variables the browser itself resolves at runtime, declared with a leading -- and read with var()). Section stylesheets use these names instead of raw numbers, so changing a token changes every section at once. The file is long but falls into a few categories.

The spacing scale runs from --space-1 (4px) to --space-15 (160px), with half steps like --space-4-5 filling gaps. Layout tokens follow: --section-spacing is the vertical padding most sections divide by two or four, --container-padding-x is the left and right page gutter, and --header-height is what MainLayout uses for its top offset.

A token can reference another, as --header-padding-x does on line 156.

Responsive overrides. Instead of every section writing its own media query for spacing, the tokens themselves shrink: below 1400px, 1200px, 992px, 768px and 480px the same names get smaller values. A section that says padding: var(--section-spacing) automatically tightens on small screens.

One subtlety: these overrides use max-width: 992px, while the tablet-down mixin uses max-width: 991px, so at exactly 992px wide the tablet token values are active but tablet-down rules are not.

src/design-system/styles/_mixins.scss 156 lines

Sass mixins for media queries. A mixin is a reusable chunk of Sass you paste in with @include; @content marks where the caller's block goes. This file contains only variables and mixins, so importing it outputs no CSS by itself, which is why every section module can safely @import it.

Lines 1–8
// ─── Breakpoint Variables ────────────────────────────────────────────────────

$breakpoint-small-mobile: 500px; // NEW
$breakpoint-mobile: 768px;
$breakpoint-tablet: 992px;
$breakpoint-desktop: 1025px;
$breakpoint-wide: 1440px;

The breakpoint widths as Sass variables. Unlike CSS custom properties, Sass variables exist only at compile time and are replaced by their values. That matters, because media queries cannot read var().

Lines 10–31
// ─── Media Query Mixins ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────

// Small Mobile (≤500px)
@mixin small-mobile {
  @media (max-width: #{$breakpoint-small-mobile - 1px}) {
    @content;
  }
}

@mixin small-mobile-up {
  @media (min-width: $breakpoint-small-mobile) {
    @content;
  }
}


// Existing Mobile (≤767px)
@mixin mobile {
  @media (max-width: #{$breakpoint-mobile - 1px}) {
    @content;
  }
}

#{$breakpoint-mobile - 1px} is Sass interpolation: it does the math at compile time and prints 767px. The "down" style mixins use max-width one pixel below a breakpoint and the "up" mixins use min-width at the breakpoint, so a mobile rule and a mobile-up rule never both apply.

Lines 40–66
// Tablet (768px – 991px)
@mixin tablet {
  @media (min-width: $breakpoint-mobile) and (max-width: #{$breakpoint-tablet - 1px}) {
    @content;
  }
}

@mixin tablet-down {
  @media (max-width: #{$breakpoint-tablet - 1px}) {
    @content;
  }
}

@mixin tablet-up {
  @media (min-width: $breakpoint-tablet) {
    @content;
  }
}


// Laptop (992px – 1279px) — small laptops and iPad Pro portrait (1024–1032px), where
// tablet-down layouts have switched off but full desktop layouts don't fit yet.
@mixin laptop {
  @media (min-width: $breakpoint-tablet) and (max-width: 1279px) {
    @content;
  }
}

tablet is a band (768px to 991px), tablet-down is everything under 992px, and tablet-up is 992px and wider. tablet-down is the mixin landing sections use most.

laptop covers 992px to 1279px, a range where tablet layouts have switched off but full desktop layouts can still be cramped (the comment mentions iPad Pro portrait). Its upper bound is a hard coded 1279px, not a variable.

Lines 69–88

  }
}

  @media (max-width: #{$breakpoint-desktop - 1px}) {
    @content;
  }
}


// Wide

  @media (min-width: $breakpoint-wide) {
    @content;
  }
}

desktop starts at 1025px, not 992px, so there is a band between tablet-up and desktop. The FAQ uses desktop to switch to two columns, so it stays stacked until 1025px. wide is 1440px and up.

Lines 93–115
@mixin respond-to($breakpoint) {
  @if $breakpoint == 'small-mobile' {
    @include small-mobile { @content; }
  } @else if $breakpoint == 'small-mobile-up' {

    @include mobile-up { @content; }
  } @else if $breakpoint == 'tablet' {
    @include tablet { @content; }
  } @else if $breakpoint == 'tablet-down' {
    @include tablet-down { @content; }
  } @else if $breakpoint == 'tablet-up' {
    @include tablet-up { @content; }
  } @else if $breakpoint == 'desktop' {
    @include desktop { @content; }
  } @else if $breakpoint == 'desktop-down' {
    @include desktop-down { @content; }
  } @else if $breakpoint == 'wide' {
    @include wide { @content; }
  }
}

respond-to lets you pass the breakpoint name as a string, as in @include respond-to('tablet-down'). It simply forwards to the named mixins; note that it has no branch for laptop.

src/lib/ghost.ts 229 lines

The Ghost CMS client. Ghost is the blogging platform that stores the posts; its Content API is read only and needs a URL and a key. The landing page uses only getFeaturedPosts and toSummary; the other helpers (getPosts, getAllPosts, getPostBySlug, getAllSlugs, getPublicTags, getRelatedPosts) serve the blog pages.

Lines 1–5

import type { BlogSummary } from '@/data/blogs'

The first comment is a warning: this file must only run on the server, because it reads a secret key from process.env. It uses require instead of import because the Ghost package ships without TypeScript types; the ESLint comment silences the rule that would otherwise complain.

Lines 68–87
// ── Client (lazy) ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
// Initialized on first use so missing env vars don't throw at module load time.

let _ghost: ReturnType<typeof GhostContentAPI> | null = null

function getClient() {
  if (!_ghost) {
    if (!process.env.NEXT_GHOST_API_URL || !process.env.NEXT_GHOST_CONTENT_API_KEY) {
      throw new Error(
        'Ghost env vars missing. Set NEXT_GHOST_API_URL and NEXT_GHOST_CONTENT_API_KEY in .env.local'
      )
    }
    _ghost = GhostContentAPI({
      url: process.env.NEXT_GHOST_API_URL,
      key: process.env.NEXT_GHOST_CONTENT_API_KEY,
      version: 'v5.0',
    })
  }
  return _ghost
}

A lazy singleton: the client is created the first time getClient() is called and reused afterwards. Creating it on first use rather than at module load means a missing environment variable only throws when someone actually fetches posts, not merely when the file is imported.

pages/index.tsx line 185 checks the same two variables first, so on the landing page this throw path is never reached.

Lines 89–109
// ── Adapter: Ghost post → BlogSummary ─────────────────────────────────────────

export function toSummary(post: GhostPost, views = 0): BlogSummary {
  return {
    slug: post.slug,
    title: post.title,
    excerpt: post.custom_excerpt ?? post.excerpt ?? '',
    image: post.feature_image ?? '',
    imageAlt: post.feature_image_alt ?? post.title,
    category: post.primary_tag?.name ?? 'General',
    categorySlug: post.primary_tag?.slug ?? '',
    publishedAt: post.published_at,
    readingTime: post.reading_time ?? 1,
    views,
    author: {
      name: post.primary_author?.name ?? 'Endless Domains',
      avatar: post.primary_author?.profile_image ?? null,
    },
    tags: (post.tags ?? []).filter(t => t.visibility === 'public').map(t => t.name),
  }
}

toSummary is an adapter: it converts the Ghost shape into the app's own BlogSummary. The ?? operator picks the right side only when the left is null or undefined, so every field gets a safe default (a custom excerpt beats the automatic one; a post without a primary tag becomes "General"). Only public tags are kept, which hides Ghost internal tags such as #featured.

Lines 153–169
export async function getFeaturedPosts(): Promise<GhostPost[]> {
  try {
    const posts: BrowseResult = await getClient().posts.browse({
      limit: 5,
      include: INCLUDE,
      filter: 'tag:hash-featured',
      order: ORDER_NEWEST,
    })
    // Fall back to newest 5 if no featured posts are tagged
    if (!posts.length) {
      return getClient().posts.browse({ limit: 5, include: INCLUDE, order: ORDER_NEWEST })
    }
    return posts
  } catch {
    return getClient().posts.browse({ limit: 5, include: INCLUDE, order: ORDER_NEWEST })
  }
}

Asks Ghost for the five newest posts carrying the internal #featured tag (Ghost spells it hash-featured in filters). If none are tagged, or the request fails, it falls back to the five newest posts overall. If the fallback fails too, the error reaches the catch in getStaticProps.

src/lib/views.ts 62 lines

View counts are not stored in Ghost; they come from the Endless backend. This file builds the URLs and wraps the requests so failures never break the page.

Lines 1–28
import axios from 'axios'

// ── Blog API path constants ───────────────────────────────────────────────────

const BLOG_BASE = '/blog'

export const BLOG_PATHS = {
  recordView: (slug: string) => `${BLOG_BASE}/views/${slug}`,
  batchViews: `${BLOG_BASE}/views`,
  popular:    `${BLOG_BASE}/popular`,
} as const

const API_BASE = `${process.env.apiDomainUrl}/${process.env.apiUrl}/${process.env.apiVersion}`

export async function getBatchViews(slugs: string[]): Promise<Record<string, number>> {
  if (!slugs.length) return {}
  try {
    const res = await fetch(
      `${API_BASE}${BLOG_PATHS.batchViews}?slugs=${slugs.join(',')}`,
      { next: { revalidate: 60 } } as RequestInit,
    )
    if (!res.ok) return {}
    const body = await res.json()
    return (body?.data as Record<string, number>) ?? {}
  } catch {
    return {}
  }
}

API_BASE is assembled from values that next.config.js copies into process.env at build time. getBatchViews sends all slugs in one request and returns a map such as { "my-post": 120 }. Every failure path returns {}, and pages/index.tsx line 196 then uses ?? 0, so a broken view service simply shows 0 views.

The { next: { revalidate: 60 } } option is an App Router fetch caching feature; inside a pages router getStaticProps it has no effect, so the 60 second rhythm really comes from the revalidate value returned in index.tsx.

Lines 48–62
// ── Client-side helper ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

// POST /blog/views/:slug — fire-and-forget beacon, call in useEffect on article page.
export function recordView(slug: string): void {
  const url = `${API_BASE}${BLOG_PATHS.recordView(slug)}`
  console.log('[recordView] POST', url)
  axios
    .post(url)
    .then(res => {
      console.log('[recordView] success —', res.status, res.data)
    })
    .catch(err => {
      console.error('[recordView] failed —', err?.response?.status, err?.response?.data ?? err?.message)
    })
}

recordView is the browser side counterpart, called from the blog article page, not the landing page. It fires a POST and does not wait for the answer.

src/lib/blog-utils.ts 35 lines

Two small pure helpers (functions that compute a result only from their inputs, with no side effects).

Lines 1–27
import type { BlogSummary } from '@/data/blogs'

const PAGE_SIZE = 9

export interface PaginatedResult<T> {
  items: T[]
  totalPages: number
  currentPage: number
  total: number
}

export function paginateBlogs(
  posts: BlogSummary[],
  page: number,
  pageSize = PAGE_SIZE
): PaginatedResult<BlogSummary> {
  const total = posts.length
  const totalPages = Math.max(1, Math.ceil(total / pageSize))
  const validPage = Math.max(1, Math.min(page, totalPages))
  const start = (validPage - 1) * pageSize
  return {
    items: posts.slice(start, start + pageSize),
    totalPages,
    currentPage: validPage,
    total,
  }
}

paginateBlogs slices a list into pages for the blog index and clamps the requested page into a valid range. The landing page does not use it.

Lines 29–35
export function formatDate(iso: string): string {
  return new Date(iso).toLocaleDateString('en-US', {
    year: 'numeric',
    month: 'long',
    day: 'numeric',
  })
}

formatDate turns an ISO timestamp like 2026-03-01T10:00:00.000Z into "March 1, 2026". BlogCard.tsx calls it for every card. It formats in the current time zone, so a post published near midnight could show a different day in the server HTML than in the browser, and React would then warn about a hydration mismatch.

src/data/blogs.ts 19 lines

Despite living in src/data, this file now holds almost no data. It is the single source of the BlogSummary type.

Lines 1–19
// Shape used by all blog UI components. Populated at build-time via src/lib/ghost.ts.

export interface BlogSummary {
  slug: string
  title: string
  excerpt: string
  image: string
  imageAlt: string
  category: string
  categorySlug: string
  publishedAt: string
  readingTime: number
  views: number
  author: { name: string; avatar: string | null }
  tags: string[]
}

// Kept as an empty array so any lingering static imports don't break the build.
export const BLOGS: BlogSummary[] = []

BlogSummary is the contract between the data layer and the UI: ghost.ts produces it, pages/index.tsx passes an array of it down, and DomainBlogTeaser and BlogCard read it. avatar: string | null forces the card to handle authors without a photo. The empty BLOGS array survives only so old imports still compile.

Flow: From build to a visitor's screen

  1. The build starts. next build finds pages/index.tsx, sees that it exports getStaticProps (pages/index.tsx:184), and calls it once on the build machine.
  2. Posts are fetched. With credentials present, getFeaturedPosts (src/lib/ghost.ts:153) returns up to five posts, getBatchViews (src/lib/views.ts:15) adds view counts, and toSummary (src/lib/ghost.ts:91) shapes each one into a BlogSummary.
  3. Static HTML is rendered. Next.js renders the provider tree from pages/_app.tsx:132 around Home with those props, inside the shell from pages/_document.tsx:7. Effects do not run on the server, so this HTML reflects initial state only: the scroll to top button is absent, roadmap cards are invisible, FAQ answers have height 0 from CSS. The HTML and a JSON file holding { blogPosts } are saved.
  4. A visitor requests the page. The server or CDN sends the saved HTML immediately; no Ghost call happens. The browser parses it, starts the preconnects and async font downloads (pages/_document.tsx:19), applies the CSS, and paints real content before any JavaScript runs.
  5. Hydration. The JavaScript bundles arrive and React hydrates: it renders the same tree again in memory using the embedded props, then attaches event handlers to the existing HTML instead of rebuilding it. That is why server and browser output must match.
  6. Effects start. Now useEffect callbacks run: the page adds its scroll listener (pages/index.tsx:109) and the subscriber timer; _app loads wallet adapters and schedules the idle session check (pages/_app.tsx:122); MainLayout restores the cart (src/design-system/layouts/mainLayout/index.tsx:96); sections start their own work, such as the roadmap IntersectionObserver and the partners marquee ticker. Scripts marked afterInteractive and lazyOnload load around this time.

Flow: Incremental regeneration: what happens every 60 seconds

  1. A freshness window is set. After a successful build, revalidate: 60 (pages/index.tsx:200) tells Next.js the cached page counts as fresh for 60 seconds.
  2. Fresh window. Every request during those 60 seconds gets the cached HTML instantly, however many visitors arrive.
  3. The first stale request. The first request after 60 seconds still receives the old cached page immediately (nobody waits), but it also triggers getStaticProps to run again in the background on the server.
  4. Background rebuild. Ghost and the views API are queried again; new posts or updated view counts become new props and new HTML, which replace the cache when done.
  5. Next visitors see the update. Requests after the rebuild get the new page, and the clock restarts. Nothing happens if nobody visits: regeneration is triggered by traffic, not by a timer.
  6. Failure handling. If Ghost throws, the catch at pages/index.tsx:202 returns an empty list with revalidate: 30, so the new page has no blog section and a retry happens on traffic after 30 seconds. Without credentials (pages/index.tsx:185) the placeholders are used with the same 30 second window.

Flow: Where a style value comes from

  1. A section writes a rule. BlogCard.module.scss:204 declares .contentInner with padding: var(--space-4) var(--space-4) var(--space-6), and at BlogCard.module.scss:211 adds @include tablet-down with smaller padding.
  2. Sass expands the mixin. The file imports _mixins.scss on line 1. At compile time tablet-down (_mixins.scss:47) becomes @media (max-width: 991px) { ... }, with 991px calculated from $breakpoint-tablet - 1px. The var() calls are left untouched, because Sass does not know their values.
  3. Next.js renames the class. Because the file ends in .module.scss, .contentInner becomes a unique name built from the file name, the class name and a short hash, along the lines of BlogCard_contentInner__x7Q2a. The styles object imported in BlogCard.tsx maps styles.contentInner to that generated string, so classes from different sections never collide.
  4. The browser resolves the variable. When the element renders, the browser looks up --space-4, finds it on :root at tokens.scss:119 (loaded globally through main.scss by pages/_app.tsx:55), and uses 16px.
  5. Responsive values stack. Under 992px the media query switches the padding to --space-3 (12px). Tokens like --section-spacing also shrink on their own through the overrides at tokens.scss:344 onward, so a section can tighten on mobile without writing any media query at all.

Hero: IdentityOsHero and the Input primitive

The first screen of the landing page: a bracketed eyebrow label, a big headline whose middle word types itself, a short paragraph, a glowing search box with a scrolling row of domain endings under it, and a "see how it works" button. Decorative rotating circles and two slanted loading bars sit behind it. IdentityOsHero.tsx is only a layout shell; each visible piece is its own small component in the same folder, and the search box itself is the shared Input primitive.

Read in this order

  1. src/design-system/composites/landing-page/IdentityOsHero/index.ts: tells you what the folder exposes to the rest of the app
  2. src/design-system/composites/landing-page/IdentityOsHero/IdentityOsHero.tsx: the map of the whole section; every other file is one of its children
  3. src/design-system/composites/landing-page/IdentityOsHero/TypeWord.tsx: the headline animation, small and self contained
  4. src/design-system/composites/landing-page/IdentityOsHero/TypeWord.module.scss: one rule, read right after TypeWord.tsx
  5. src/design-system/composites/landing-page/IdentityOsHero/SearchBar.tsx: the search area that wraps the Input
  6. src/design-system/primitives/Input/index.ts: one more barrel file, a few seconds
  7. src/design-system/primitives/Input/Input.tsx: where a search actually happens
  8. src/design-system/primitives/Input/Input.module.scss: how the glowing search box is drawn
  9. src/design-system/composites/landing-page/IdentityOsHero/SearchBar.module.scss: layout and the marquee; skip lines 117 to 386
  10. src/design-system/composites/landing-page/IdentityOsHero/SeeHowItWorksButton.tsx: a tiny button, quick win
  11. src/design-system/composites/landing-page/IdentityOsHero/SeeHowItWorksButton.module.scss: visual polish only
  12. src/design-system/composites/landing-page/IdentityOsHero/StripeAnimation.tsx: pure decoration, read once you know the layout
  13. src/design-system/composites/landing-page/IdentityOsHero/BoxLoader.tsx: more decoration, with a timer loop like TypeWord
  14. src/design-system/composites/landing-page/IdentityOsHero/IdentityOsHero.module.scss: the shell styles tie everything together last

src/design-system/composites/landing-page/IdentityOsHero/index.ts 1 lines

A barrel file is a small index.ts whose only job is to reexport things, so other code can import from the folder name instead of a long file path. This one is imported by src/design-system/composites/landing-page/index.ts.

Lines 1–1
export { IdentityOsHero, default } from './IdentityOsHero'

It forwards both the named export IdentityOsHero and the default export, so a default import and a named import of the folder both work.

src/design-system/composites/landing-page/IdentityOsHero/IdentityOsHero.tsx 49 lines

The hero shell. It has no state and no logic of its own; it simply arranges the decorative layers, the headline, the search bar and the button in the right order.

Lines 1–10
import styles from './IdentityOsHero.module.scss'
import BoxLoader from './BoxLoader'
import StripeAnimation from './StripeAnimation'
import SearchBar from './SearchBar'
import SeeHowItWorksButton from './SeeHowItWorksButton'
import TypeWord from './TypeWord'

// "reputation" leads so server-rendered, no-JS, and pre-hydration text all match what
// the page already used before this cycled — the other three only show up client-side.
const HERO_WORDS = ['reputation', 'identity', 'name', 'presence'] as const

A CSS module is a .scss file whose class names are made unique per file at build time; importing it gives you an object, so styles.heroSection is the real, renamed class string. That is why two files can both have a class called .searchContainer without clashing.

HERO_WORDS lists the words the headline cycles through. as const tells TypeScript the array will never change, so its type becomes a fixed list of these exact strings. The comment explains the ordering: the page is first rendered on the server (before any JavaScript runs), and the server always shows the first word, so "reputation" goes first to match the older static headline.

Lines 12–37
export function IdentityOsHero() {
  return (
    <section className={styles.heroSection}>
      <BoxLoader interval={100} pauseDuration={800} gap={5} />
      <BoxLoader interval={100} pauseDuration={800} gap={5} position="right" />

      <div className={styles.animationBg}>
        <StripeAnimation />
        <div className={styles.content_wrapper}>
          <div className={styles.subheading}>
            <h4>The OS for onchain identity</h4>
            <span></span>
          </div>
          <h1>
            <span aria-hidden="true">
              <span>Own your</span> <br />
              <TypeWord words={HERO_WORDS} className={styles.txt_gradient} /> forever.
            </span>
            {/* Real accessible text lives outside .txt_gradient — its background-clip: text nests badly with hidden fallback content. */}
            <span className={styles.srOnly}>Own your reputation forever.</span>
          </h1>
          <p>
            Your email, social media, and payment methods all belong to someone else. One domain on Endless changes that. No landlord. No platform that can revoke it. Own your identity starting from $2.
          </p>
        </div>
      </div>

Top to bottom: two BoxLoader decorations (one on the left, one mirrored on the right, configured through props, which are the attributes you pass to a component), then a wrapper holding StripeAnimation behind the text.

The headline is built twice on purpose. The visible copy (line 26) has aria-hidden="true", which hides it from screen readers (software that reads the page aloud for blind users), because a word that keeps changing letter by letter would be read out as gibberish. The hidden copy on line 31 uses the srOnly class: it is invisible on screen but read aloud as one calm sentence.

TypeWord receives the word list and the txt_gradient class so the typed word is painted in brand blue.

      <div className={styles.content}>
        <SearchBar />
        <div className={styles.ctaWrap}>
          <SeeHowItWorksButton />
        </div>
      </div>
    </section>
  )
}

export default IdentityOsHero

The lower half: the search bar, then the call to action button in its own centring wrapper. The file exports the component twice (named and default) so the barrel file can forward both.

src/design-system/composites/landing-page/IdentityOsHero/IdentityOsHero.module.scss 393 lines

Styles for the shell plus the two decorations that import this same file (BoxLoader.tsx and StripeAnimation.tsx). Line 1 imports the shared mixins: a mixin is a reusable chunk of Sass, and here each one wraps a media query, so @include tablet-down means "only when the screen is narrower than 992px". The breakpoints live in src/design-system/styles/_mixins.scss (500, 768, 992, 1025 and 1440px). Lines 5 to 19 (the stripe layer) are covered under StripeAnimation; the classes .animationTrack and .strokePath are not used by any component.

.wrapper hides both box loaders below 992px, since there is no room for decoration on tablets and phones. .left and .right give each loader a 44px tall strip and push it down by a percentage of the viewport height (vh means one percent of the window height) so it sits above the dip of the stripe circles. The inner svg rule sizes the gradient outline drawn by BoxLoader.

Each box is skewed (skewX) so the row looks like a slanted progress bar: 30 degrees one way on the left, the opposite way on the right. When BoxLoader adds the active class, the box turns solid blue at full opacity.

Beginner gotcha: line 147 asks for an animation called popRight, but no @keyframes popRight exists in this file, so that line has no visible effect. The box simply snaps to blue.

Lines 154–174
.heroSection {
  position: relative;
  width: 100%;
  overflow: hidden;
  // Top spacing is intentionally untouched — this section sits directly below the page
  // header, a different boundary case handled elsewhere. Only the bottom half of the
  // shared --section-spacing gap belongs here, to meet IdentityOsProvidersStats halfway.
  padding-bottom: calc(var(--section-spacing) / 2);

  // On tall narrow phones (e.g. iPhone 16 Pro Max) the hero's own content is shorter
  // than the viewport, so the next section peeks in on first load instead of the hero
  // filling the screen. 100dvh (not 100vh) so mobile browser chrome showing/hiding
  // doesn't leave a gap or force extra scroll. Desktop/tablet are left as pure
  // content-driven height, unchanged.
  @include tablet-down {
    min-height: calc(100dvh - var(--header-height));
    display: flex;
    flex-direction: column;
    justify-content: center;
  }
}

The section is the positioning parent for its absolute children and clips anything that overflows (the big circles). Its bottom padding is half of the shared gap between sections, so it "meets the next section halfway".

On tablets and phones the hero gets a minimum height of the full screen minus the header, using 100dvh. The dvh unit is the dynamic viewport height, which follows the phone browser's address bar as it shows and hides, unlike plain vh. Flexbox then centres the content vertically.

.ctaWrap centres the button and, on desktop, pulls it up by 30px with a negative margin to cancel empty space left inside the search area. Below 992px it goes back to a normal gap.

.content_wrapper stacks the eyebrow, headline and paragraph in a column. The rules are written mobile first: the base values are for the smallest screens, and each @include block overrides them as the screen gets wider.

Lines 295–321

// ─── Gradient / solid text ────────────────────────────────────────────────────

.txt_gradient {
  background: var(--color-blue-primary);
  background-clip: text;
  -webkit-background-clip: text;
  -webkit-text-fill-color: transparent;
}

.txt_solid {
  background: var(--color-blue-primary);
  background-clip: text;
  -webkit-background-clip: text;
  -webkit-text-fill-color: transparent;
}

.srOnly is the standard "visually hidden" recipe: a 1px box with everything clipped, still present for screen readers.

.txt_gradient paints text with a background: background-clip: text cuts the background to the shape of the letters and the transparent text fill lets it show through. Here the background is a single blue, so the effect is solid blue text. .txt_solid is an identical copy that this hero does not use.

The bracketed eyebrow badge. The four corner marks are drawn with pseudo elements: ::before and ::after are extra boxes CSS can add to any element without extra HTML. The div provides two corners and the empty span inside it (stretched over the badge with inset: 0) provides the other two. Each mark is a 10px square with only two of its four borders kept, which makes an L shape.

src/design-system/composites/landing-page/IdentityOsHero/TypeWord.tsx 81 lines

A self contained component that shows one word at a time and animates it like someone typing: pause, delete letter by letter, type the next word, repeat.

Lines 1–21
import { useEffect, useRef, useState } from 'react'
import styles from './TypeWord.module.scss'

interface TypeWordProps {
  words: readonly string[]
  typingSpeed?: number
  deletingSpeed?: number
  pauseDuration?: number
  className?: string
}

export default function TypeWord({
  words,
  typingSpeed = 90,
  deletingSpeed = 45,
  pauseDuration = 1400,
  className,
}: TypeWordProps) {
  const [text, setText] = useState(words[0])
  const indexRef = useRef(0)
  const timeoutRef = useRef<NodeJS.Timeout | null>(null)

The props interface lists what the parent may pass; the defaults (90ms per typed letter, 45ms per deleted letter, 1400ms pause) apply when the parent passes nothing.

State is data that, when changed, makes React redraw the component: useState(words[0]) starts with the first word, which is also what the server renders. A ref made with useRef is a box that keeps a value between redraws without causing a redraw. indexRef remembers which word we are on and timeoutRef remembers the current timer so it can be cancelled.

Lines 23–43
  useEffect(() => {
    const reducedMotion =
      typeof window !== 'undefined' && window.matchMedia('(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)').matches
    let cancelled = false

    const wait = (ms: number) =>
      new Promise<void>(resolve => {
        timeoutRef.current = setTimeout(resolve, ms)
      })

    const run = async () => {
      if (reducedMotion) {
        // Whole-word swap, no per-letter animation, honoring the OS-level preference.
        while (!cancelled) {
          await wait(pauseDuration + typingSpeed * 6)
          if (cancelled) return
          indexRef.current = (indexRef.current + 1) % words.length
          setText(words[indexRef.current])
        }
        return
      }

useEffect runs code after the component appears in the browser (never on the server). First it checks the operating system "reduce motion" setting with matchMedia.

wait wraps setTimeout in a Promise, an object representing a value that arrives later. Inside an async function you can write await wait(500) and the code pauses there for half a second, which lets the animation read like a simple story instead of nested callbacks. The timer id is saved in timeoutRef so cleanup can cancel it.

If reduced motion is on, the loop just swaps the whole word every couple of seconds with no letter by letter effect.

Lines 45–68
      while (!cancelled) {
        const current = words[indexRef.current]

        await wait(pauseDuration)
        if (cancelled) return

        for (let i = current.length; i >= 0; i--) {
          if (cancelled) return
          setText(current.slice(0, i))
          await wait(deletingSpeed)
        }

        indexRef.current = (indexRef.current + 1) % words.length
        const next = words[indexRef.current]

        for (let i = 0; i <= next.length; i++) {
          if (cancelled) return
          setText(next.slice(0, i))
          await wait(typingSpeed)
        }
      }
    }

    run()

The main loop. It waits, then deletes the current word one character at a time by calling setText with ever shorter slices (slice(0, i) keeps the first i letters). Then it moves to the next word; the % (remainder) operator wraps the index back to 0 after the last word. Finally it types the new word one letter at a time.

cancelled is checked after every wait, so the loop can stop cleanly. run() starts the loop without awaiting it.

Lines 70–81
    return () => {
      cancelled = true
      if (timeoutRef.current) clearTimeout(timeoutRef.current)
    }
  }, [words, typingSpeed, deletingSpeed, pauseDuration])

  return (
    <span className={`${styles.slot} ${className ?? ''}`}>
      <span aria-hidden="true">{text}</span>
    </span>
  )
}

The function returned from useEffect is the cleanup: React calls it when the component is removed from the page, or before rerunning the effect because a dependency changed. It flips cancelled and clears the pending timer, so no stray setText runs on a component that no longer exists.

The dependency array on line 74 lists the values the effect relies on; if any changes, React cleans up and starts a fresh loop. The rendered text is aria-hidden for the same screen reader reason explained in IdentityOsHero.tsx.

src/design-system/composites/landing-page/IdentityOsHero/TypeWord.module.scss 7 lines

One rule for the slot that holds the typed word.

Lines 1–7
.slot {
  display: inline-block;
  text-align: left;
  // Room for descenders (y, g, p) so background-clip: text doesn't crop them; negative margin keeps line spacing unchanged.
  padding-bottom: 0.2em;
  margin-bottom: -0.2em;
}

inline-block lets the word sit inside the sentence while still accepting padding. The 0.2em bottom padding gives letters like y, g and p room so the text clipped background does not cut off their tails, and the equal negative margin cancels the extra space so the line height does not change.

src/design-system/composites/landing-page/IdentityOsHero/SearchBar.tsx 76 lines

Everything around the search box: two layers of background art, the shared Input component, and below it a scrolling strip of domain endings plus a link to the full list.

Lines 1–9
import Image from 'next/image'
import Link from 'next/link'
import styles from './SearchBar.module.scss'
import Input from '@/design-system/primitives/Input'

const DOMAIN_EXTENSIONS = [
  '.og', '.eth', '.sol', '.bnb', '.crypto', '.x', '.arb', '.polygon', '.ton', '.tez',
  '.bitcoin', '.nft', '.wallet', '.dao', '.blockchain', '.zil', '.stark', '.box', '.hodl', '.onchain',
] as const

next/image is the Next.js image component; it resizes and lazy loads images automatically. next/link makes client side links that change pages without a full reload. Input is the shared search primitive from the design system.

DOMAIN_EXTENSIONS is the list of TLDs (top level domains, the part after the last dot such as .eth) shown in the marquee.

Two background images use fill, which makes the image cover its positioned parent. priority and fetchPriority="high" tell the browser to download them immediately because they are visible on first load. There are two versions of the search background, a mobile .avif and a desktop .svg, and CSS hides whichever does not fit the screen. The decorative images have empty alt text and aria-hidden, so screen readers skip them.

<Input className={styles.wideSearch} /> renders the real search form. The extra class only sets a CSS variable that widens this one instance (see the SCSS file).

Lines 47–70
        <div className={styles.domainList}>
          {/* Same footprint the static pills used; the list renders twice so the
              translateX(-50%) loop is seamless — the copy is hidden from screen readers. */}
          <div className={styles.marquee} role="list" aria-label="Popular domain extensions">
            <div className={styles.marqueeTrack}>
              {[...DOMAIN_EXTENSIONS, ...DOMAIN_EXTENSIONS].map((domain, index) => {
                const isCopy = index >= DOMAIN_EXTENSIONS.length

        </div>

The marquee. The list is spread twice into one array ([...A, ...A]), so 40 pills render: the first 20 are real list items and the copies get aria-hidden so screen readers do not read every TLD twice. Keys combine the domain and the index because each domain appears twice. Why two copies are needed is explained in the marquee flow below.

Next to it, a Link labelled "60+ TLDs" goes to /web3-domains, with a small inline arrow icon.

src/design-system/composites/landing-page/IdentityOsHero/SearchBar.module.scss 576 lines

Layout of the search area, the marquee and the glowing "60+ TLDs" link. Important for beginners: lines 117 to 386 (.searchWrapper, .searchContainer, .inputContent, .searchInput, .searchButton with its spinner and label rules, and the spin keyframes) are leftover styles copied from an older version of the search box. You can skip that whole block. The real versions of those rules live in Input.module.scss.

Lines 7–13
// Widens just this hero's search input, without touching the shared Input primitive's
// default width used on the other 5 pages that render it — Input.module.scss's
// .searchContainer reads this custom property (falling back to its own default when
// it isn't set), so setting it here only affects instances rendered inside this class.
.wideSearch {
  --search-container-max-width: 780px;
}

A CSS custom property (a variable whose name starts with two dashes) set on a parent is inherited by everything inside it. Input.module.scss sizes its box with var(--search-container-max-width, 600px), meaning "use the variable if it is set, otherwise 600px". Setting it to 780px here widens only the hero search box without touching the other pages that use Input.

Lines 35–61
.search_bg {
  position: relative;
  z-index: 1;
  // width: clamp(320px, 90%, 760px);
      width: clamp(320px, 95%, 850px);
  // height: clamp(180px, 19vw, 333px);
  height: clamp(180px, 25vw, 350px);
  margin: auto;
  display: flex;
  justify-content: center;
  align-items: center;
  flex-direction: column;    transform: translateY(-20%);
  @include desktop-down {
      transform: translateY(20%);
  }

  // Below tablet the pills can wrap onto two rows, so a fixed height + translateY hack
  // (tuned for a single row) no longer clears the button underneath. Sized to its actual
  // content instead — the fill background images below size to whatever height this
  // resolves to, so the art still fills the box exactly.
  @include tablet-down {
    width: 100%;
    height: auto;
    min-height: 220px;
    padding: var(--space-6) 0 var(--space-4);
    transform: none;
  }

The box that holds the background art, the input and the pills. clamp(min, preferred, max) picks the preferred value but never goes outside the limits, so the width follows the screen yet stays between 320px and 850px. On desktop the content is nudged up 20% with transform; below 1025px it is nudged down instead.

Below 992px the fixed height and nudge are dropped (height: auto, transform: none) because the pills may wrap onto two rows. Lines 71 to 77 cap the height on large desktops. Line 46 squeezes two declarations onto one line; it is just formatting.

  initial-value: 0deg;
}

These two rules are used, by the .domainMore link. @property registers --border-angle as a real angle. Normally the browser cannot animate a custom property because it does not know its type; once it knows it is an angle, the keyframes can smoothly turn it from 0 to 360 degrees.

The link gets a rotating rainbow border with a two layer background trick. The first layer is a plain colour clipped to the padding-box (inside the border); the second is a conic gradient (colours arranged around a centre point, like a colour wheel) clipped to the border-box, so it only shows through the transparent 2px border. Animating --border-angle spins the wheel, so the border colours seem to travel around the pill. A blurred copy behind it (::before, lines 528 to 546) makes the glow, hover scales the link up, and lines 569 to 575 stop all of it for users who prefer reduced motion.

src/design-system/composites/landing-page/IdentityOsHero/StripeAnimation.tsx 155 lines

Draws four huge thin circles behind the headline, with glowing strokes that sweep around them forever. It uses only SVG and its built in animateTransform, so React never rerenders it. The container style (.animationContainer, lines 5 to 19 of IdentityOsHero.module.scss) places it absolutely behind the content, one screen tall, ignoring clicks.

Lines 4–30
const R = 561.812;
const CIRCUMFERENCE = 2 * Math.PI * R;

// c0/c1 each get exactly one stroke that starts right at the top edge of the hero
// (y=200) and sweeps downward into the visible dip, looping forever — instead of the
// leading+trailing blade pair.
// c0: starts at 37.7° (x≈523, y=200 — the top edge). Sweeping with *increasing* angle
// moves it downward (37.7°→90°, the dip's lowest point, y=418) before continuing on.
// c1 mirrors this at 142.3° (x≈996, y=200) — sweeping with *decreasing* angle moves it
// downward the same way (142.3°→90°, the dip's lowest point).
// c2/c3 (bottom corners) are untouched — original leading+trailing blade pair.
const CIRCLES = [
  { id: 'c0', cx: -11.515,  cy: -143.812, startDeg: 37.7,  dir: 1,  single: true  },
  { id: 'c1', cx: 1530.112, cy: -143.812, startDeg: 142.3, dir: -1, single: true  },
  { id: 'c2', cx: 78.485,   cy: 837.763,  startDeg: 270,   dir: 1,  single: false },
  { id: 'c3', cx: 1438.112, cy: 837.763,  startDeg: 90,    dir: 1,  single: false },
] as const;

const DUR = '5s';

const DASH_A = 160;
const GAP_A  = CIRCUMFERENCE - DASH_A;

const DASH_B = 160;
const GAP_B  = CIRCUMFERENCE - DASH_B;

const STAGGER = 15;

R is the circle radius and CIRCUMFERENCE its full length. CIRCLES places four circles: two centred above the top edge (c0, c1) and two below (c2, c3). startDeg is where the stroke begins, dir is the direction (1 clockwise, negative 1 anticlockwise), and single says whether the circle gets one glowing blade or two.

DASH_A and GAP_A drive strokeDasharray, which draws a line as "dash, gap, dash, gap". A 160 unit dash followed by a gap equal to the rest of the circumference means exactly one short visible blade per circle.

Lines 32–57
export default function StripeAnimation() {
  return (
    <div className={styles.animationContainer}>
      <svg
        xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg"
        viewBox="0 200 1512 600"
        fill="none"
        className={styles.animationSvg}
        aria-hidden="true"
      >
        <defs>

        </defs>

        {/* ── Static track circles ── */}
        {CIRCLES.map(({ id, cx, cy }) => (
          <circle
            key={id}
            cx={cx}
            cy={cy}
            r={R}
            stroke="#e4e4e480"
            strokeWidth="1"
            fill="none"
          />
        ))}

The SVG viewBox chooses which part of the drawing coordinates is visible (from y=200, 1512 wide, 600 tall), and the SVG scales to fill its container. The first map draws each circle as a faint grey static track.

Lines 69–106
          return (
            <g key={`blades-${id}`}>
              {/* Blade A — leading, brighter */}
              <g filter="url(#glow)">
                {/* Rotate the entire group: gradient + circle together */}
                <g>
                  <animateTransform
                    attributeName="transform"
                    type="rotate"
                    from={`${startDeg} ${cx} ${cy}`}
                    to={`${startDeg + dir * 360} ${cx} ${cy}`}
                    dur={DUR}
                    repeatCount="indefinite"
                  />

// …
                  <circle
                    cx={cx}
                    cy={cy}
                    r={R}
                    stroke={`url(#grad-${id}-a)`}
                    strokeWidth="6"
                    strokeLinecap="round"
                    strokeDasharray={`${DASH_A} ${GAP_A}`}
                    fill="none"
                  />
                </g>

For each circle a group is rotated by animateTransform from startDeg to one full turn later around the circle centre, every 5 seconds, forever. Because the gradient and the dashed circle are inside the same rotating group, the fade of the blade (transparent tail, solid head) travels with it.

Note for readers: filter="url(#glow)" points to a filter that does not exist, since the <defs> block on lines 42 to 44 is empty, so no glow filter is applied; the browser just ignores it.

Lines 109–122
              {/* Blade B — trailing, softer. c0/c1 skip this: a single blade reads as
                  one unambiguous stroke per circle instead of two separately-visible
                  glows. c2/c3 keep the original leading+trailing pair. */}
              {!single && (
                <g filter="url(#glow-soft)">
                  <g>
                    <animateTransform
                      attributeName="transform"
                      type="rotate"
                      from={`${startDeg - STAGGER} ${cx} ${cy}`}
                      to={`${startDeg - STAGGER + dir * 360} ${cx} ${cy}`}
                      dur={DUR}
                      repeatCount="indefinite"
                    />

Only the bottom circles (single: false) get a second, trailing blade that starts 15 degrees (STAGGER) behind the first, giving a comet with a softer echo. The comment explains why the top two keep only one.

src/design-system/composites/landing-page/IdentityOsHero/BoxLoader.tsx 130 lines

A decorative loading bar: ten slanted boxes light up one after another, pause, reset, and start again. Its styles live in IdentityOsHero.module.scss. The 'use client' line at the top is a marker used by the newer Next.js app router; in this pages router project it changes nothing.

Lines 21–38
export default function BoxLoader({
  count = 10,
  interval = 150,
  pauseDuration = 600,
  gap = 5,
  position = 'left',
  className,
}: BoxLoaderProps) {
  const [activeCount, setActiveCount] = useState(0)
  const timerRef = useRef<ReturnType<typeof setTimeout> | null>(null)
  const fillingRef = useRef(true)

  const clearTimer = useCallback(() => {
    if (timerRef.current) {
      clearTimeout(timerRef.current)
      timerRef.current = null
    }
  }, [])

Default props make the loader work with no configuration; the hero passes faster values (interval={100}). activeCount (state) is how many boxes are lit, so changing it redraws the boxes. timerRef holds the pending timer and fillingRef remembers whether we are filling or about to reset; refs are used because changing them should not cause a redraw.

useCallback keeps the same clearTimer function between redraws, so it is safe to list in the effect dependencies.

Lines 40–63
  useEffect(() => {
    const step = () => {
      if (fillingRef.current) {
        setActiveCount(prev => {
          const next = prev + 1
          if (next >= count) {
            fillingRef.current = false
            timerRef.current = setTimeout(step, pauseDuration)
            return next
          }
          timerRef.current = setTimeout(step, interval)
          return next
        })
      } else {
        setActiveCount(0)
        fillingRef.current = true
        timerRef.current = setTimeout(step, interval)
      }
    }

    timerRef.current = setTimeout(step, interval)

    return clearTimer
  }, [count, interval, pauseDuration, clearTimer])

step is a function that schedules itself again with setTimeout, creating a loop. While filling, it adds one to activeCount (using the updater form prev => prev + 1, which always receives the latest value). After the last box it waits pauseDuration, then resets to 0 and starts over.

Returning clearTimer as the cleanup stops the loop when the component leaves the page. One subtlety: the timer is scheduled inside the state updater, which React expects to be a pure function; in development Strict Mode React may call updaters twice, so this pattern can occasionally schedule an extra step. It is harmless for a decoration.

Lines 65–130
  const isLeft = position === 'left'
  const gradientId = `stroke-gradient-${position}`

  return (
    <div className={`${styles.wrapper} ${isLeft ? styles.left : styles.right} ${className ?? ''}`}>
      {/* SVG border stroke */}
      {isLeft ? (
        <svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="542" height="44" viewBox="0 0 542 44" fill="none">
// …
      {/* Boxes container with clipped shape */}
      <div className={styles.boxesContainer}>
        <div className={styles.loader} style={{ gap: `${gap}px` }}>
          {Array.from({ length: count }, (_, i) => {
            const index = isLeft ? i : count - 1 - i
            return <div key={i} className={`${styles.box} ${index < activeCount ? styles.active : ''}`} />
          })}
        </div>
      </div>
    </div>
  )
}

The gradient outline path is chosen by position (elided SVG markup). Then Array.from({ length: count }) creates count boxes. For the right hand loader the index is reversed (count - 1 - i) so boxes light from the right. A box gets the active class when its index is below activeCount. The gap prop becomes an inline style.

src/design-system/primitives/Input/Input.tsx 238 lines

The shared search input used by the hero and several other pages. It has two looks (hero and filter). In the hero look it animates its placeholder, cleans what you type, validates on submit, and navigates to the search page. Imports on lines 1 to 7 bring in React hooks, the Next.js router, a search icon, and the toast helper used for error messages.

Lines 11–36
const PLACEHOLDER_DOMAINS = [
  'explorer.og',
  'beneath.eth',
  'hello.sol',
  'naruto.anime',
  'crypto.wallet',
  'defi.chain',
  'web3.bitcoin',
  'digital.nft',
  'metaverse.og',
  'blockchain.eth',

const TYPING_SPEED = 100
const DELETING_SPEED = 50
const PAUSE_DURATION = 2000

// ─── Types ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

export interface InputProps extends React.InputHTMLAttributes<HTMLInputElement> {
  variant?: 'hero' | 'filter'
  showExtensions?: boolean
  initialQuery?: string
  onSearch?: (query: string) => void
  placeholderDomains?: readonly string[]
}

The sample domains that the placeholder types out, and the typing timings. InputProps extends the normal HTML input attributes, so the component accepts anything a real <input> accepts plus its own options. onSearch lets a parent handle the search itself instead of navigating. showExtensions is declared but never used in the component.

Lines 40–68
export const Input = forwardRef<HTMLInputElement, InputProps>(
  (
    {
      variant = 'hero',
      showExtensions = true,
      initialQuery = '',
      onSearch,
      placeholderDomains = PLACEHOLDER_DOMAINS,
      className,
      ...inputProps
    },
    ref,
  ) => {
    const router = useRouter()

    const [query, setQuery] = useState(initialQuery)
    const [isSearching, setIsSearching] = useState(false)
    const [isFocused, setIsFocused] = useState(false)
    const [placeholder, setPlaceholder] = useState('')
    const [currentDomainIndex, setCurrentDomainIndex] = useState(0)
    const [isDeleting, setIsDeleting] = useState(false)

    const typingRef = useRef<NodeJS.Timeout | null>(null)

    useEffect(() => {
      const urlQuery = router.query.query as string | undefined
      const resolved = initialQuery || urlQuery || ''
      if (resolved) setQuery(resolved)
    }, [initialQuery, router.query.query])

forwardRef lets a parent pass a ref that lands on the inner <input> element, so the parent could focus it directly. Destructuring pulls out the custom props and collects the rest into inputProps with ....

The state: the typed query, whether a search is in progress, whether the box is focused, and the pieces of the placeholder animation. The first effect reads ?query= from the URL (via useRouter) so the box is prefilled when you land on /search?query=hello.

Lines 72–107
    useEffect(() => {
      if (variant !== 'hero') return

      if (isFocused || query) {
        setPlaceholder('Search your Web3 digital identity')
        return
      }

      const currentDomain = placeholderDomains[currentDomainIndex % placeholderDomains.length]
      const currentText = placeholder

      const tick = () => {
        if (!isDeleting) {
          if (currentText.length < currentDomain.length) {
            setPlaceholder(currentDomain.slice(0, currentText.length + 1))
            typingRef.current = setTimeout(tick, TYPING_SPEED)
          } else {
            typingRef.current = setTimeout(() => setIsDeleting(true), PAUSE_DURATION)
          }
        } else {
          if (currentText.length > 0) {
            setPlaceholder(currentText.slice(0, -1))
            typingRef.current = setTimeout(tick, DELETING_SPEED)
          } else {
            setIsDeleting(false)
            setCurrentDomainIndex(prev => (prev + 1) % placeholderDomains.length)
          }
        }
      }

      typingRef.current = setTimeout(tick, isDeleting ? DELETING_SPEED : TYPING_SPEED)

      return () => {
        if (typingRef.current) clearTimeout(typingRef.current)
      }
    }, [placeholder, currentDomainIndex, isDeleting, isFocused, query, variant, placeholderDomains])

The placeholder animation, written differently from TypeWord. Instead of a loop, each tick changes state (placeholder, isDeleting, currentDomainIndex), the state change rerenders the component, and because those values are in the dependency array the effect runs again and schedules the next tick. The cleanup clears the previous timer each time.

When the box is focused or has text, the animation stops and a fixed hint "Search your Web3 digital identity" is shown.

Lines 111–143
    const handleSearch = useCallback(
      async (e: FormEvent<HTMLFormElement>) => {
        e.preventDefault()
        if (isSearching) return

        const formData = new FormData(e.currentTarget)
        const queryText = (formData.get('searchText') as string)?.trim()

        if (!queryText) return

        // Reject anything except letters, numbers and dots — handleInputChange
        // already strips these live, but this covers input that bypasses it
        // (e.g. a paste or programmatic form submit).
        if (!/^[a-zA-Z0-9.]+$/.test(queryText)) {
          ToastMessage(TOAST_TYPE.ERROR, '', 'Only letters, numbers, and dots are allowed.')
          return
        }

        setIsSearching(true)
        try {
          if (onSearch) {
            onSearch(queryText)
          } else {
            await router.push(`/search?query=${encodeURIComponent(queryText)}`)
          }
        } catch {
          // navigation errors are non-fatal
        } finally {
          setIsSearching(false)
        }
      },
      [router, isSearching, onSearch],
    )

Runs when the form is submitted (Enter key or button click). e.preventDefault() stops the browser default full page form submission. FormData reads the field whose name is searchText, and trim() removes spaces.

The regular expression (a pattern for matching text) /^[a-zA-Z0-9.]+$/ accepts only letters, digits and dots. Anything else shows an error toast (a small popup message) and stops. Otherwise isSearching is set, which disables the form, and router.push moves to /search?query=.... encodeURIComponent makes the text safe inside a URL. finally always switches searching off again.

Lines 145–162
    const handleInputChange = useCallback((e: React.ChangeEvent<HTMLInputElement>) => {
      const sanitizedValue = e.target.value.replace(/[^a-zA-Z0-9.]/g, '')
      setQuery(sanitizedValue)
    }, [])

    const handleFocus = useCallback(() => {
      setIsFocused(true)
      setPlaceholder('Search your Web3 digital identity')
    }, [])

    const handleBlur = useCallback(() => {
      setIsFocused(false)
      if (!query) {
        setPlaceholder('')
        setCurrentDomainIndex(0)
        setIsDeleting(false)
      }
    }, [query])

This is a controlled input: React state (query) is the single source of truth and the input shows value={query}. Every keystroke runs handleInputChange, which deletes disallowed characters with replace before saving, so typing a space or a symbol simply does nothing. Focus shows the fixed hint; leaving an empty box resets the animation to the first domain.

    height: 100%;
    background: linear-gradient(90deg, transparent, var(--color-white-alpha-40), transparent);
    transition: left 0.7s ease;
    z-index: 1;
    pointer-events: none;
  }

  &:not(:disabled):hover {
    background: var(--color-black-hover);
    box-shadow: 0 4px 12px var(--color-blue-primary-alpha-30);

    &::after {
      left: 100%;
    }

    .icon {
      color: var(--color-white-primary);
    }

    .labelUp {
      top: -100%;
    }
  }

Hover effects. A white gradient stripe (::after) waits just off the left edge (left: -100%); on hover it slides to the right over 0.7s, making a glass shimmer. At the same time the icon turns white and both label copies move up by their own height (top: -100%). :not(:disabled) makes sure none of this happens while searching. Pressing the button shrinks it slightly.
