The odometer itself. .digitReel is exactly 1em tall with overflow: hidden, a window one character high. .digitStrip stacks the twenty cells in a column and animates any transform change over 0.48 seconds with a smooth ease out curve. Each .digitCell is also 1em, which is why moving by 5% of the strip moves exactly one digit. The blue gradient text fill is applied on each cell rather than once on the parent because the comment notes that the clipped background failed to paint through the transformed strip.

Lines 586–606
@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) {
  .digitStrip { transition: none; }

  .dataPacket[data-phase='live'],

    transition: none;
  }
}

For reduced motion users the digit transition is removed, packets show statically, and the shimmer and fade ins are switched off. Worth knowing: with no transition, the transitionend event that OdometerDigit waits for never fires, so after a 9 to 0 rollover the strip stays in the second copy and is never snapped back. After a second rollover posRef reaches 20, past the last cell, and that reel would show blank. In normal motion this cannot happen.

Flow: One counter tick, digit by digit

  1. The section becomes active. The observer at DomainOnchainStats.tsx:119 sets active to true when a quarter of the section is visible; CSS fades in the title and decor.
  2. The ticker starts. The effect at DomainOnchainStats.tsx:126 runs because active changed, and schedules the first tick after 0.5 to 1.4 seconds (line 140).
  3. Count and pulse. tick (line 132) adds 1 or 2 at line 134, turns pulse on at line 135 and off 220ms later at line 136, then schedules itself again at line 137.
  4. The number is split. On rerender, line 148 formats the count with commas and line 149 splits it into characters. Say 10,009,869 becomes 10,009,870.
  5. Only changed digits react. Each digit is keyed by its position from the right (line 195), so the same OdometerDigit instances are reused. Their effect (line 23) only runs where the digit prop changed: here the tens (6 to 7) and the ones (9 to 0).
  6. Normal step. For the tens, the new digit is bigger, so line 31 moves the position forward by 1 and line 33 writes the new translateY. The CSS transition at DomainOnchainStats.module.scss:394 slides the strip up one cell. Since the position is below 10, line 35 returns.
  7. Rollover step. For the ones, 9 to 0 is smaller, so line 31 moves forward into the second copy (position 9 becomes 10, the second "0"). The strip slides one cell further, still in the same direction, so it looks like a real wheel turning.
  8. Snap back. Because the position is now 10 or more, a transitionend listener is attached at line 45. When the slide finishes, lines 37 to 39 turn the transition off and jump to position 0, which shows the same "0", and lines 40 to 42 turn the transition back on in the next frame. The listener removes itself at line 43.
  9. Repeat or stop. The next tick follows 0.7 to 2.3 seconds later. When the section scrolls out of view, active becomes false and the cleanup at lines 141 to 145 stops the loop.

The Adoption Gap

This is the white section with the headline "741 Million People Hold Onchain Assets. Almost None Own an Identity.", a long gray bar with a tiny glowing blue slice at its left end, and a big "Under 2%" statistic underneath. Everything is in one React component that plays a one time reveal the first time you scroll to it: the text fades up, the total counts to 741M, the blue slice grows, and a few small pulses fire. The timeline lives in JavaScript as a list of timers that flip boolean flags, and the SCSS turns each flag (exposed as a data-* attribute) into a CSS transition.

src/design-system/composites/landing-page/DomainAdoptionGap/DomainAdoptionGap.tsx 187 lines

Renders the whole section and owns its animation timeline. It watches whether the section is on screen, latches that into a "has been seen" flag, then schedules a sequence of timers that each switch one visual detail on or off.

Lines 10–32
function useQuickCount(run: boolean, steps: number[], stepDelayMs: number, instant: boolean) {
  const [value, setValue] = useState(steps[0])
  useEffect(() => {
    if (!run) return
    if (instant) {
      setValue(steps[steps.length - 1])
      return
    }
    let cancelled = false
    steps.forEach((step, i) => {
      setTimeout(() => {
        if (!cancelled) setValue(step)
      }, i * stepDelayMs)
    })
    return () => {
      cancelled = true
    }
  }, [run, instant])
  return value
}

const TOTAL_STEPS = [0, 174, 421, 741]
const PERCENT_STEPS = [0, 1, 2]

A custom hook is just a function whose name starts with use and that calls other React hooks, so a piece of stateful logic can be reused. useQuickCount holds a number in state and, once run becomes true, schedules one setTimeout per step (step 0 at 0 ms, step 1 at 130 ms, and so on), so the number jumps 0, 174, 421, 741.

The function returned from a useEffect is its cleanup: React calls it before the effect runs again or when the component is removed. Here the cleanup does not cancel the timers; it sets cancelled = true, and each timer checks that flag before touching state. If the user prefers reduced motion (instant), it skips the steps and shows the final value straight away.

Lines 34–58
export function DomainAdoptionGap() {
  const sectionRef = useRef<HTMLElement>(null)
  const [active, setActive] = useState(false)
  // Flips false → true the first time the section is seen and never resets — unlike
  // `active`, which toggles back to false the moment the section scrolls out of view
  // again. The reveal effect below depends on THIS, not `active`, so a fast scroll that
  // crosses the 30% threshold and back out within a couple of seconds can't cancel the
  // timers it already scheduled (see the bug this fixes, below).
  const [triggered, setTriggered] = useState(false)
  const [revealed, setRevealed] = useState(false)
  const hasRunRef = useRef(false)
  const [reducedMotion, setReducedMotion] = useState(false)
  const [barBuilt, setBarBuilt] = useState(false)
  const [boundaryPulse, setBoundaryPulse] = useState(false)
  const [labelReveal, setLabelReveal] = useState(false)
  const [grayPulse, setGrayPulse] = useState(false)
  const [percentSettled, setPercentSettled] = useState(false)
  const [countRun, setCountRun] = useState(false)
  const [percentRun, setPercentRun] = useState(false)

  const total = useQuickCount(countRun, TOTAL_STEPS, 130, reducedMotion)
  // stepDelayMs (275) × the 4 intervals between PERCENT_STEPS' 5 values = 1100ms — tuned
  // to match .barFill's own 1.1s CSS transition exactly, so the number finishes counting
  // at the same instant the bar finishes filling instead of trailing it by a second.
  const percent = useQuickCount(percentRun, PERCENT_STEPS, 275, reducedMotion)

Instead of one big "animation progress" number, the component keeps one boolean per visual effect: is the bar built, is the boundary pulsing, is the label revealed, and so on. Each boolean later becomes a data-* attribute in the JSX, which keeps the CSS very readable.

useRef gives you a box whose .current value survives rerenders without causing one when it changes. sectionRef points at the real <section> DOM element and hasRunRef remembers "the reveal already ran".

Note the comment on lines 55 to 57: it talks about five percent steps, but PERCENT_STEPS on line 32 now has three values, so the percent finishes in 2 × 275 = 550 ms rather than 1100 ms. Comments can drift away from the code they describe, so always trust the code.

Lines 60–76
  useEffect(() => {
    if (typeof window === 'undefined') return
    setReducedMotion(window.matchMedia('(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)').matches)
  }, [])

  useEffect(() => {
    if (typeof window === 'undefined' || !sectionRef.current) return
    const observer = new IntersectionObserver(entries => entries.forEach(entry => setActive(entry.isIntersecting)), { threshold: 0.3 })
    observer.observe(sectionRef.current)
    return () => observer.disconnect()
  }, [])

  // Latches `active` into a one-way flag — see the `triggered` declaration above for why
  // the reveal effect needs this instead of depending on `active` directly.
  useEffect(() => {
    if (active) setTriggered(true)
  }, [active])

The first effect reads the operating system "reduce motion" setting with window.matchMedia. The typeof window === 'undefined' guard exists because Next.js also renders the page on the server, where there is no window.

An IntersectionObserver is a browser API that calls you back when an element enters or leaves the viewport. With threshold: 0.3 it fires when 30% of the section is visible, and setActive mirrors that true or false. The cleanup observer.disconnect() stops watching when the component unmounts.

The third effect is a latch: once active is true, triggered becomes true and never goes back. That is what protects the timeline from being cancelled by a quick scroll in and out.

Lines 91–122
  useEffect(() => {
    if (!triggered || hasRunRef.current) return
    hasRunRef.current = true
    setRevealed(true)

    if (reducedMotion) {
      setCountRun(true)
      setPercentRun(true)
      setBarBuilt(true)
      setLabelReveal(true)
      setPercentSettled(true)
      return
    }

    const timers: ReturnType<typeof setTimeout>[] = []
    const at = (ms: number, fn: () => void) => timers.push(setTimeout(fn, ms))

    at(500, () => setCountRun(true))
    // Bar starts filling and the percent counter starts climbing together — both tuned to
    // land at t=2000 (900 + the bar's 1.1s transition), so the fill and the number always
    // finish as one connected motion instead of drifting apart.
    at(900, () => setBarBuilt(true))
    at(900, () => setPercentRun(true))
    at(2000, () => setBoundaryPulse(true))
    at(2050, () => setPercentSettled(true))
    at(2250, () => setBoundaryPulse(false))
    at(2350, () => setLabelReveal(true))
    at(2550, () => setGrayPulse(true))
    at(3150, () => setGrayPulse(false))

    return () => timers.forEach(clearTimeout)
  }, [triggered, reducedMotion])

This is the heart of the section. The guard lets the body run only once. setRevealed(true) starts the CSS fade ins, then the small helper at(ms, fn) schedules a timer and stores its id in an array so the cleanup can clear them all.

Read the at(...) calls as a script: the count starts at 500 ms, the bar and percent at 900 ms, the boundary glows from 2000 to 2250 ms, the percent text settles to "Under 2%" at 2050 ms, the labels brighten at 2350 ms and the gray area pulses from 2550 to 3150 ms. With reduced motion, the early return jumps every flag straight to its final value.

The long comment above it explains a real bug: when this effect depended on active, scrolling out mid reveal reran the effect, its cleanup cleared all timers, and the guard blocked rescheduling, so the bar froze at 0%. Depending on triggered fixes it.

Each flag is passed as data-x={flag || undefined}. When the flag is false the expression is undefined, and React leaves the attribute out completely; when true, the attribute appears. CSS can then target [data-built] without caring about its value.

The bar is three layers inside .barTrack: the gray "anonymous" area, the blue fill with its boundary line, and a floating label. aria-hidden="true" hides the decorative bar from screen readers, and the visually hidden .srOnly span says the same facts in words instead.

The big statistic shows the live percent while counting and switches to the words "Under 2%" once percentSettled becomes true. &apos; is how JSX writes an apostrophe without upsetting the linter.

The blue fill is drawn at its final width and squashed to nothing with transform: scaleX(0). transform-origin: left center makes it grow from the left edge. When data-built appears it scales to 1 over 1.1s. Animating transform instead of width is cheaper because the browser does not have to recalculate the page layout every frame.

The first background layer is a repeating gradient of thin white ticks, so as the bar grows the ticks appear one after another for free.

Flow: The reveal timeline after the section scrolls into view

  1. The observer notices the section. When 30% of it is visible, the callback sets active to true (DomainAdoptionGap.tsx:67).
  2. The latch flips. The small effect at DomainAdoptionGap.tsx:75 sets triggered to true, permanently.
  3. The reveal effect starts. Its guard passes once, it marks hasRunRef and calls setRevealed(true) (DomainAdoptionGap.tsx:92 to 94), which puts data-active on the section (DomainAdoptionGap.tsx:125).
  4. The header cascades in. CSS fades the eyebrow, heading, description, labels and bar track in with growing delays (DomainAdoptionGap.module.scss:41, 78, 122, 153, 191).
  5. At 500 ms the total counts. setCountRun(true) (DomainAdoptionGap.tsx:108) makes useQuickCount step through 0, 174, 421, 741 every 130 ms (DomainAdoptionGap.tsx:19), shown in the "M total" label (DomainAdoptionGap.tsx:150).
  6. At 900 ms the bar grows. barBuilt and percentRun both turn on (DomainAdoptionGap.tsx:112 to 113); .barFill[data-built] scales from 0 to 1 over 1.1s (DomainAdoptionGap.module.scss:261) while the percent counts 0, 1, 2.
  7. At 2000 ms the boundary glows. boundaryPulse turns on (DomainAdoptionGap.tsx:114, styled at DomainAdoptionGap.module.scss:301) and off again at 2250 ms (DomainAdoptionGap.tsx:116).
  8. At 2050 ms the number settles. percentSettled swaps the digits for the words "Under 2%" (DomainAdoptionGap.tsx:115, 172).
  9. At 2350 ms the labels brighten. labelReveal lifts the blue label to full opacity and shows "10M+ with an identity" (DomainAdoptionGap.module.scss:170, 280).
  10. From 2550 to 3150 ms the gray area pulses. grayPulse briefly brightens the anonymous region (DomainAdoptionGap.tsx:118 to 119, DomainAdoptionGap.module.scss:214), then the section rests with only the slow ambient scan running.
  11. Reduced motion and cleanup. With reduced motion every flag is set at once (DomainAdoptionGap.tsx:96); if the component unmounts mid timeline, the cleanup clears every stored timer (DomainAdoptionGap.tsx:121).

Domain Growth Comparison

Two cards side by side compare the mature Web2 domain resale market ($290M, a white card with a bar chart) with the young Web3 identity resale market ($3.86M, a blue card with a growth line). The charts start hidden and animate in together: on a desktop with a mouse they appear while the pointer is over the cards, and on touch screens they appear once when the cards scroll into view. The file is organised as one custom hook that decides "are the charts active", two small chart components that receive that flag, and the section component that lays out the cards.

Read in this order

  1. index.ts: confirms the exported names.
  2. DomainGrowthComparison.tsx, lines 1 to 74: the data constants and useHoverOrViewportActive, because the single active flag drives everything else.
  3. DomainGrowthComparison.tsx, the rest: how BarChart and GrowthLine turn that flag into attributes and inline delays.
  4. DomainGrowthComparison.module.scss: the transitions that actually move things.

src/design-system/composites/landing-page/DomainGrowthComparison/DomainGrowthComparison.tsx 274 lines

Holds the chart data, decides when the charts are active, and draws both cards. No animation runs in JavaScript; the code only switches attributes and inline transitionDelay values, and CSS does the motion.

Lines 6–25
// Matches the Figma frame's own 4-bar chart exactly — a short-medium-tallest-medium
// silhouette, not an invented trend line. Percent of the chart's own max height,
// derived from the source frame's own bar heights (97 / 115 / 187 / 142 px).
const BAR_HEIGHTS = [52, 62, 100, 76]

// The two data points the growth line passes through, extracted from the Figma frame's
// own marker positions, expressed as a fraction of the chart's viewBox so the curve is
// grounded in the real design rather than an invented shape.
const LINE_VIEWBOX = { width: 564, height: 220 }
const LINE_PATH = 'M 4 202 C 90 196, 170 190, 232 166 C 292 140, 352 122, 407 103 C 452 87, 500 48, 540 12'
const LINE_POINTS: Array<{ x: number; y: number; label: string; value?: string; side: 'above' | 'below'; align: 'center' | 'end' }> = [
  // Right-aligned onto its own point (instead of centered) so its pill sits further left,
  // clear of the second point's callout right next to it — centered, the two pills
  // overlapped since the points themselves are close together near the chart's top-right.
  { x: 407, y: 103, label: 'Web3 Identity', side: 'above', align: 'end' },
  // This point sits right at the top-right corner of the chart — a callout floating
  // above and centered on it would clip against both the top and right edges of the
  // card, so it drops below the point and right-aligns onto it instead.
  { x: 540, y: 12, label: 'Web3 Identity', side: 'below', align: 'end' },
]

The chart data is a few plain constants at the top of the file. BAR_HEIGHTS are percentages of the chart height. LINE_PATH is an SVG path string: M moves the pen to a point and each C draws a smooth cubic curve, all in the coordinate system of a 564 by 220 viewBox (the internal drawing grid of the SVG).

LINE_POINTS lists the two highlighted dots with a typed shape. side and align say where each callout pill sits relative to its dot, so labels near the top right corner are not clipped.

Lines 27–43
function useHoverOrViewportActive() {
  const groupRef = useRef<HTMLDivElement>(null)
  const [active, setActive] = useState(false)
  const [supportsHover, setSupportsHover] = useState(true)
  const [reducedMotion, setReducedMotion] = useState(false)

  useEffect(() => {
    if (typeof window === 'undefined') return
    // (hover: hover) alone isn't reliable — some mobile browsers report it as true even
    // on a plain touchscreen, which would route mobile down the hover-only path below and
    // require a tap before anything shows. Below the same tablet breakpoint the rest of
    // the site treats as "mobile" (992px), always force viewport activation instead.
    const hasFineHover = window.matchMedia('(hover: hover) and (pointer: fine)').matches
    const isMobileWidth = window.matchMedia('(max-width: 991px)').matches
    setSupportsHover(hasFineHover && !isMobileWidth)
    setReducedMotion(window.matchMedia('(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)').matches)
  }, [])

The hook starts with three pieces of state. On mount it asks the browser two questions with matchMedia: does this device have a precise pointer that can hover (a mouse), and is the window narrower than 992px? Only a mouse on a wide screen counts as "supports hover". The comment explains why: some phones claim hover support, which would otherwise leave the charts hidden until a tap.

Lines 53–74
  useEffect(() => {
    if (supportsHover || typeof window === 'undefined' || !groupRef.current) return
    const observer = new IntersectionObserver(
      entries => {
        entries.forEach(entry => {
          if (entry.isIntersecting) {
            setActive(true)
            observer.disconnect()
          }
        })
      },
      { threshold: 0.15 },
    )
    observer.observe(groupRef.current)
    return () => observer.disconnect()
  }, [supportsHover])

  const handleEnter = () => supportsHover && setActive(true)
  const handleLeave = () => supportsHover && setActive(false)

  return { groupRef, active, reducedMotion, handleEnter, handleLeave }
}

On touch devices an IntersectionObserver (a browser API that reports when an element enters the viewport) watches the card group. As soon as 15% of it is visible it sets active to true and immediately disconnects, so the charts reveal once and stay revealed. The effect lists supportsHover as a dependency, so it reruns after the first effect has measured the device.

On desktop the observer is skipped and the two handlers do the work: entering the group sets active true and leaving sets it false. supportsHover && setActive(true) is a short way of writing "only if hover is supported".

Lines 83–95
function BarChart({ active }: BarChartProps) {
  return (
    <div className={styles.barChart} data-active={active || undefined} aria-hidden="true">
      {BAR_HEIGHTS.map((height, i) => (
        <span
          key={i}
          className={styles.bar}
          style={{ height: `${height}%`, transitionDelay: active ? `${i * 90}ms` : `${(BAR_HEIGHTS.length - i) * 40}ms` }}
        />
      ))}
    </div>
  )
}

BarChart renders one <span> per height. Each bar gets its height inline and a staggered transitionDelay: growing in, bar i waits i × 90 ms so the bars sweep left to right; shrinking back, the delay is reversed ((4 − i) × 40 ms) so they collapse right to left, a little faster.

Lines 104–138
function GrowthLine({ active }: GrowthLineProps) {
  const pathRef = useRef<SVGPathElement>(null)
  const [pathLength, setPathLength] = useState(1)

  useLayoutEffect(() => {
    if (pathRef.current) setPathLength(pathRef.current.getTotalLength())
  }, [])

  return (
    <div className={styles.lineChart} data-active={active || undefined} aria-hidden="true">
      <span className={styles.lineGrid} />
      <svg className={styles.lineSvg} viewBox={`0 0 ${LINE_VIEWBOX.width} ${LINE_VIEWBOX.height}`} preserveAspectRatio="none" fill="none">
        <defs>
          <linearGradient id="growthLineFill" x1="0" y1="0" x2="0" y2="1">
            <stop offset="0%" stopColor="var(--color-white-alpha-40)" />
            <stop offset="100%" stopColor="var(--color-white-alpha-08)" />
          </linearGradient>
        </defs>
        <path
          className={styles.lineFill}
          d={`${LINE_PATH} L 540 220 L 4 220 Z`}
          fill="url(#growthLineFill)"
        />
        <path
          ref={pathRef}
          className={styles.linePath}
          d={LINE_PATH}
          stroke="var(--color-white-primary)"
          strokeWidth={2}
          strokeLinecap="round"
          style={{
            strokeDasharray: pathLength,
            strokeDashoffset: active ? 0 : pathLength,
          }}
        />

The line "draws itself" with a classic SVG trick. stroke-dasharray splits a stroke into dashes; if one dash is as long as the whole path, and stroke-dashoffset shifts it by that same length, the line is invisible. Transitioning the offset to 0 reveals it from start to end.

To know the length, useLayoutEffect calls getTotalLength() on the real path element. useLayoutEffect is like useEffect but runs right after the DOM is updated and before the browser paints, so the correct length is in place before anything is shown. The first <path> is the soft filled area under the curve, closed back to the bottom with L ... Z.

Lines 139–165
        {LINE_POINTS.map((point, i) => (
          <circle
            key={i}
            className={styles.linePoint}
            data-active={active || undefined}

            r={4}
            fill="var(--color-white-primary)"

          }}

The dots and callouts are timed to appear after the line has passed them: the dots wait 450 ms and 700 ms, the callouts 600 ms and 850 ms. When deactivating, the delay is 0 so they vanish at once. Callouts are HTML positioned over the SVG; their left and top are percentages computed from the viewBox coordinates, so they line up with the dots at any card size.

Hovering the group lifts the cards with a soft shadow, and the reduced motion attribute applies the same shadow permanently. Inside the prefers-reduced-motion media query the transitions are switched off, so when the charts become active they appear instantly rather than animating.

Flow: Hover on desktop vs scroll on touch: how the charts activate

  1. First render assumes hover. supportsHover starts as true and active as false, so every chart part is hidden (DomainGrowthComparison.tsx:29 to 30).
  2. The device is measured. After mount the hook checks for a fine pointer and a wide window and stores the answer (DomainGrowthComparison.tsx:39 to 41).
  3. Desktop path: the pointer enters. onMouseEnter on the group (DomainGrowthComparison.tsx:209) calls handleEnter, which sets active true (DomainGrowthComparison.tsx:70).
  4. Touch path: the cards scroll in. With hover unsupported, the observer fires at 15% visibility, sets active true and disconnects for good (DomainGrowthComparison.tsx:58 to 60).
  5. The bars stagger up. BarChart adds data-active and delays of 0, 90, 180 and 270 ms (DomainGrowthComparison.tsx:90); CSS scales each bar from 0 to 1 over 0.6s (DomainGrowthComparison.module.scss:288).
  6. The line draws. strokeDashoffset changes from the measured path length to 0 (DomainGrowthComparison.tsx:136) and the 1.1s transition reveals it from left to right (DomainGrowthComparison.module.scss:337), while the fill under it fades in (DomainGrowthComparison.module.scss:332).
  7. Dots and callouts follow the pen. Dots appear at 450 and 700 ms (DomainGrowthComparison.tsx:148), callouts at 600 and 850 ms (DomainGrowthComparison.tsx:163), and the veils glow (DomainGrowthComparison.module.scss:239).
  8. Desktop only: the pointer leaves. handleLeave sets active false (DomainGrowthComparison.tsx:71); the bars shrink with reversed delays, the dash offset slides back and the dots disappear immediately. On touch nothing is ever reversed.

Living Identity

This section shows a "network" on the left (eight TLD bubbles such as .og and .eth orbiting a lock icon), a glowing bridge with an icon in the middle, and a "Living identity" wallet panel on the right whose activity feed scrolls one row at a time. All three parts move together on a steady two second beat. The code keeps a single tick counter in the component, derives the ring angle, the flashing node and the active feed row from it, and lets CSS animate between positions; the static content (nodes, feed rows, beat length) lives in a separate data file.

Read in this order

  1. index.ts: the exported names.
  2. DomainLivingIdentityData.ts: small and concrete, it tells you what is being animated.
  3. DomainLivingIdentity.tsx: start at the component (line 92) for the tick logic, then read the JSX; read BridgeConnectorArt last, it is pure decoration.
  4. DomainLivingIdentity.module.scss: the orbit rules (lines 122 to 273) explain the ring, then the panel rules explain the feed.

// The 8 orbit nodes, evenly spaced around the ring (0° = top, clockwise) — matches the
// Figma network's own 8-point layout. Each gets its own sway/breathe timing below so the
// ring reads as an organic network, not a spinner (see .orbitSlot / .orbitArm in the scss).

  { id: 'tld-eth', label: '.eth', angle: 45, swayDuration: 18, breatheDuration: 11 },
  { id: 'tld-sol', label: '.sol', angle: 90, swayDuration: 13, breatheDuration: 8 },

]

Eight nodes, one every 45 degrees around the circle (0 is the top). Each also carries its own swayDuration and breatheDuration in seconds; because these differ, the nodes drift out of step with each other and the ring feels organic instead of mechanical. The icons imported on line 1 come from react-icons, a package that ships icons as React components.

  Icon: typeof PiWalletBold
  title: string
  desc: string
  value: string
}

// Exact copy from Figma — the four rows the activity panel cycles through.
export const ACTIVITY_ROWS: ActivityRow[] = [
  { id: 'payment', Icon: PiWalletBold, title: 'Payment received', desc: 'paid to alex.og, no address needed', value: '+0.42 ETH' },
  { id: 'parked', Icon: PiPauseCircleBold, title: 'Domain parked', desc: 'earning on offers while you hold', value: '2 offers' },
  { id: 'marketplace', Icon: PiTrendUpBold, title: 'Marketplace value rising', desc: 'digital real estate that appreciates', value: '+18%' },
  { id: 'reputation', Icon: PiSealCheckBold, title: 'Reputation building', desc: 'wallet history → portable score', value: '+38' },
]

// Fixed "ticking clock" rhythm — rotate, stop, rotate, stop, on a steady beat rather
// than the irregular random pause this used to be. Kept in one place since the ring's
// CSS transition duration (.orbitRing / .orbitNode in the stylesheet) needs to stay
// shorter than this so each tick visibly settles before the next one fires.
export const ACTIVITY_TICK_INTERVAL_MS = 2000

An interface is a TypeScript description of an object's shape. ActivityRow says each feed row has an id, an Icon component (typeof PiWalletBold means "the same type as this icon component"), a title, a description and a value. ACTIVITY_TICK_INTERVAL_MS is the beat of the whole section: 2000 ms, deliberately longer than the 0.7s CSS transitions so each move settles before the next one starts.

src/design-system/composites/landing-page/DomainLivingIdentity/DomainLivingIdentity.tsx 274 lines

Builds the three columns and runs the clock. Every moving part reads from the same tick number, so they can never fall out of sync.

Lines 22–38, 73–74, 87–89
function BridgeConnectorArt({ flipped }: { flipped?: boolean }) {
  const gradientId = `bridge-connector-${useId().replace(/:/g, '')}`
  return (
    <svg
      className={styles.connectorArt}
      viewBox="0 0 136 35"
      preserveAspectRatio="none"
      style={flipped ? { transform: 'scaleX(-1)' } : undefined}
      aria-hidden="true"
    >
      <defs>
        <linearGradient id={gradientId} x1="0" y1="0" x2="1" y2="0">
          <stop offset="0%" stopColor="#2639ED" />
          <stop offset="100%" stopColor="#2639ED" stopOpacity="0" />
        </linearGradient>
      </defs>
      <path d="M0 17.4432H136" fill="none" stroke={`url(#${gradientId})`} strokeOpacity="0.4" strokeWidth="4" />
// …
      <g className={styles.connectorGlow} style={{ animationDirection: flipped ? 'reverse' : 'normal' }}>
        <path d="M0 17.4432H136" fill="none" stroke="white" strokeWidth="3" />
// …
      </g>
    </svg>
  )

BridgeConnectorArt draws the curvy connector lines as an inline SVG. useId() is a React hook that returns an id unique to this component instance; it is needed because the left and right connectors each define a gradient, and two gradients with the same id would clash. React ids contain colons, which break the url(#id) reference, so they are stripped.

The flipped prop mirrors the art with scaleX(-1) for the left side. The <g> group with styles.connectorGlow is a bright copy of the lines revealed through a moving CSS mask; its animation runs in reverse when flipped so the glow still travels left to right on screen.

Lines 92–126
export function DomainLivingIdentity() {
  // useId()'s colons break as an SVG href="#..." fragment reference, so strip them.
  const captionPathId = `caption-circle-${useId().replace(/:/g, '')}`
  const sectionRef = useRef<HTMLElement>(null)
  const [active, setActive] = useState(false)
  const [reducedMotion, setReducedMotion] = useState(false)
  // Single source of truth for the whole system: which activity row is highlighted,
  // which orbit node flashes, and how far the ring has rotated all derive from this one
  // counter, so they can never drift out of sync with each other.
  const [tick, setTick] = useState(0)

  useEffect(() => {
    if (typeof window === 'undefined') return
    setReducedMotion(window.matchMedia('(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)').matches)
  }, [])

  useEffect(() => {
    if (typeof window === 'undefined' || !sectionRef.current) return
    const observer = new IntersectionObserver(entries => entries.forEach(entry => setActive(entry.isIntersecting)), { threshold: 0.25 })
    observer.observe(sectionRef.current)
    return () => observer.disconnect()
  }, [])

  // Ticking-clock rhythm — rotate, stop, rotate, stop, on a fixed beat — and it only
  // runs while the section is actually in view.
  useEffect(() => {
    if (!active || reducedMotion) return
    const timer = setInterval(() => setTick(t => t + 1), ACTIVITY_TICK_INTERVAL_MS)
    return () => clearInterval(timer)
  }, [active, reducedMotion])

  const activeRow = tick % ACTIVITY_ROWS.length
  const activeNodeIndex = tick % ORBIT_NODES.length
  const ringAngle = tick * (360 / ORBIT_NODES.length)
  const ActiveIcon = ACTIVITY_ROWS[activeRow].Icon

Two familiar effects come first: read the reduced motion setting, and use an IntersectionObserver (a browser API that reports when an element is on screen) to keep active true only while a quarter of the section is visible.

The clock effect starts a setInterval only while active and not in reduced motion; its cleanup clearInterval stops it when you scroll away, so nothing ticks off screen. setTick(t => t + 1) is the functional update form, which always adds to the latest value instead of a stale one captured when the interval was created.

Lines 123 to 126 derive everything from tick with the remainder operator %, which wraps the counter back to the start of each list: the feed row cycles through 4, the flashing node through 8, and the ring angle grows by 45 degrees per tick. The angle is never wrapped, so the ring always keeps turning in the same direction.

Lines 157–175
          {/* Rotates the whole set of nodes one step per tick — the "og icons rotate one
              by one" ring turn. Each .orbitNode counter-rotates by the same amount (see
              the stylesheet) so the ".og" labels stay upright as the ring turns. */}
          <div className={styles.orbitRing} style={{ '--ring-angle': `${ringAngle}deg` } as React.CSSProperties}>
            {ORBIT_NODES.map((node, i) => (
              <div
                key={node.id}
                className={styles.orbitSlot}
                style={{ '--base-angle': `${node.angle}deg`, '--sway-duration': `${node.swayDuration}s` } as React.CSSProperties}
              >
                <div className={styles.orbitArm} style={{ '--breathe-duration': `${node.breatheDuration}s` } as React.CSSProperties}>
                  <span className={styles.orbitNode}>
                    {node.label}
                    {i === activeNodeIndex && <span className={styles.orbitNodeFlash} key={tick} />}
                  </span>
                </div>
              </div>
            ))}
          </div>

Three nested layers each do one job. .orbitRing rotates the whole set by --ring-angle. Each .orbitSlot rotates to its own --base-angle and sways a few degrees. Each .orbitArm pushes the bubble out to the radius and breathes in and out. Values are passed as CSS custom properties (variables like --ring-angle) through the style prop; the as React.CSSProperties cast is needed because TypeScript does not know about custom property names.

The flash ring is rendered only on the active node and has key={tick}. Changing a key makes React treat the element as brand new, unmount the old one and mount a fresh one, which restarts its CSS animation every tick.

              — an SVG textPath rather than a flat span, so it actually follows that gap
              instead of just sitting flat behind the lock. Sized off .network (380px),
              not .identityCore, so the circle is the right diameter. */}

The faint caption "inactive domains today" follows a circle using SVG <textPath>, which lays text along a path. The bridge is connector, core, connector. The core uses the same key trick as the node flash: key={`core-${tick}`} remounts it every tick so its glow flash plays again. Inside it, ActiveIcon is the icon of the currently active feed row, so the middle icon always matches the highlighted row on the right. A component stored in a variable must start with a capital letter for JSX to treat it as a component.

Lines 235–265
            {/* A 3-row window onto the activity feed. Every row is always mounted — only
                its slot (-1 above, 0 centered, 1 below, 2 queued off-screen) changes each
                tick — so the CSS transition on transform/opacity animates it sliding
                between those positions instead of popping in and out. */}
            <div className={styles.activityViewport} role="list" aria-label="Recent wallet activity">
              {ACTIVITY_ROWS.map((row, i) => {
                const Icon = row.Icon
                const distance = ((i - activeRow) % ACTIVITY_ROWS.length + ACTIVITY_ROWS.length) % ACTIVITY_ROWS.length
                const slot = distance === ACTIVITY_ROWS.length - 1 ? -1 : distance
                return (
                  <div
                    className={styles.activityRow}
                    data-slot={slot}
                    role="listitem"
                    key={row.id}
                    style={{
                      transform: `translateY(${(slot + 1) * ACTIVITY_ROW_HEIGHT}px) scale(${slot === 0 ? 1.06 : 0.94})`,
                      opacity: slot === 2 ? 0 : slot === 0 ? 1 : 0.55,
                    }}
                  >
                    <span className={styles.activityIcon}>
                      <Icon />
                    </span>
                    <div className={styles.activityText}>
                      <p className={styles.activityTitle}>{row.title}</p>
                      <p className={styles.activityDesc}>{row.desc}</p>
                    </div>
                    <span className={styles.activityValue}>{row.value}</span>
                  </div>
                )
              })}

All four feed rows are always rendered; only their slot changes. distance is how many steps a row is after the active one, wrapped into 0 to 3 (the extra + length) % length fixes JavaScript's negative remainder). Distance 3 is turned into slot -1, meaning "just above". So slot -1 is the row above, 0 is the highlighted middle row, 1 is below and 2 is waiting out of sight.

The inline transform moves each row to (slot + 1) × 64 pixels down and scales the middle one up slightly; opacity hides slot 2. Because the same element simply gets a new transform, the CSS transition slides it to its new place.

Lines 176–205
// Rotates the entire set of nodes together, one 45° step per activity tick, with a
// snappy transition and nothing animating between ticks — the "rotate, stop, rotate,
// stop" ticking-clock motion. Wraps .orbitSlot rather than replacing its per-node sway
// below, so the ring turn and each node's own gentle sway compose instead of conflicting.
.orbitRing {
  position: absolute;
  inset: 0;
  transform: rotate(var(--ring-angle, 0deg));
  transition: transform 0.7s cubic-bezier(0.65, 0, 0.35, 1);
}

// Each orbit slot swings a few degrees around its own fixed base angle rather than
// rotating a full circle — that oscillation, not rotation, is what keeps this from
// reading as a loading spinner (requirement: "DO NOT MAKE IT LOOK LIKE A LOADING
// SPINNER"). Every node gets its own duration (from --sway-duration), so they drift out
// of phase with each other instead of moving as one mechanical unit.
.orbitSlot {
  position: absolute;
  inset: 0;
  // Static fallback — the keyframes below override this while the animation runs, but
  // if the animation is ever disabled (prefers-reduced-motion), this is what's left, and
  // it must still place the node at its correct angle rather than collapsing to identity.
  transform: rotate(var(--base-angle));
  animation: orbit-sway var(--sway-duration, 16s) ease-in-out infinite;
}

@keyframes orbit-sway {
  0%, 100% { transform: rotate(calc(var(--base-angle) - 4deg)); }
  50% { transform: rotate(calc(var(--base-angle) + 4deg)); }
}

.orbitRing reads --ring-angle from the inline style and has a 0.7s transition, so each 45 degree step animates and then the ring waits for the next tick: the "ticking clock" feel. .orbitSlot runs a keyframe animation forever that swings it 4 degrees either side of its base angle. The plain transform on line 198 is a fallback for when the animation is disabled.

The arm is a pure translate: it centers the bubble and lifts it by --radius, and its breathe animation adds 6px more at the midpoint. The long comment above it in the file explains why an earlier version with its own rotation drew a skewed path.

Each bubble then counter rotates by the ring angle plus its base angle, so the text stays upright however far the ring has turned, and it uses the same 0.7s transition so the two motions cancel out smoothly. The flash ring plays its keyframes once (1 as the iteration count), growing and fading.

@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) {

For reduced motion every ambient animation stops and the moving parts lose their transitions. Combined with the component, which never starts the interval in this mode, the section renders as one calm still frame.

Flow: One tick: ring turns, node flashes, feed advances

  1. The clock fires. Every 2000 ms (DomainLivingIdentityData.ts:40) the interval runs setTick(t => t + 1) (DomainLivingIdentity.tsx:119). It only exists while the section is on screen and motion is allowed (DomainLivingIdentity.tsx:118).
  2. Everything is derived again. On rerender, activeRow, activeNodeIndex and ringAngle are recomputed from the new tick (DomainLivingIdentity.tsx:123 to 125), and ActiveIcon is the icon of the new row (DomainLivingIdentity.tsx:126).
  3. The ring turns 45 degrees. The new --ring-angle lands on .orbitRing (DomainLivingIdentity.tsx:160) and its 0.7s transition rotates the set (DomainLivingIdentity.module.scss:183 to 184). At the same time each bubble counter rotates with the same timing to stay upright (DomainLivingIdentity.module.scss:254 to 255).
  4. The next node flashes. The flash span moves to the new active node and, because its key is the tick (DomainLivingIdentity.tsx:170), it mounts fresh and plays orbit-node-flash once (DomainLivingIdentity.module.scss:265).
  5. The bridge core pulses and swaps icon. The core's key changes (DomainLivingIdentity.tsx:204), so it remounts with the new row's icon (DomainLivingIdentity.tsx:208) and its glow flash replays (DomainLivingIdentity.module.scss:438).
  6. The feed advances one row. Each row's slot is recalculated (DomainLivingIdentity.tsx:242 to 243) and gets a new inline transform and opacity (DomainLivingIdentity.tsx:251 to 252); the 0.7s transition slides the old middle row up, the row below into the middle, and the queued row into view (DomainLivingIdentity.module.scss:605).
  7. Rest until the next beat. All transitions finish in 0.7s and the section holds still (apart from the gentle sway, breathe and glow loops) for the remaining 1.3s. Scroll away and the observer sets active false, the cleanup clears the interval, and the tick freezes until you return (DomainLivingIdentity.tsx:110, 120).

How It Works Journey

This is the "From Mint To Earning In Simple Steps" section: a row of five tabs (Mint your identity, Enable reputation, Domain parking, Marketplace, Perks) above a large frame that shows the chosen journey's title on the left and a numbered app screenshot with a title and description on the right. The screenshots advance on their own every 1200 ms, and every change of content fades out and back in. The content is pure data in journeyData.ts, the fade logic is a reusable hook in useCrossfade.ts, the right hand panel is its own small component, and HowItWorksJourney.tsx wires tabs, timer and crossfades together.

FileLinesRole
src/design-system/composites/landing-page/HowItWorksJourney/index.ts2Barrel file that reexports the component
src/design-system/composites/landing-page/HowItWorksJourney/journeyData.ts198Types and content for the five journeys and their screens
src/design-system/composites/landing-page/HowItWorksJourney/useCrossfade.ts41Hook that turns a value change into exit, enter start and idle phases
src/design-system/composites/landing-page/HowItWorksJourney/JourneyScreenVisual.tsx43Right hand panel: screenshot (or placeholder), number, title and description
src/design-system/composites/landing-page/HowItWorksJourney/HowItWorksJourney.tsx130Section component: tab state, auto advance timer, keyboard tabs, crossfades and layout
src/design-system/composites/landing-page/HowItWorksJourney/HowItWorksJourney.module.scss433Styles for both components, including the phase driven transitions and breakpoints

Read in this order

  1. index.ts: the exported names.
  2. journeyData.ts: the types tell you exactly what a journey and a screen contain.
  3. useCrossfade.ts: the one tricky piece of logic; understanding its three phases makes the rest easy.
  4. JourneyScreenVisual.tsx: a simple presentational component that receives a screen and a phase.
  5. HowItWorksJourney.tsx: ties it all together.
  6. HowItWorksJourney.module.scss: match each data-phase value to its CSS.

src/design-system/composites/landing-page/HowItWorksJourney/useCrossfade.ts 41 lines

A generic hook that delays showing a new value until the old one has faded out, and reports which phase of the animation you are in, without ever remounting the DOM element.

Lines 1–19
import { useEffect, useRef, useState } from 'react'

export type CrossfadePhase = 'idle' | 'exit' | 'enter-start'

// Drives a two-step exit-then-enter transform/opacity swap without a DOM
// remount: the OLD content plays its exit transition, then — once it's fully
// hidden — the NEW value takes over the same node, jumps (no transition) to
// an offset starting position, and on the very next frame the phase flips
// back to 'idle', which re-enables the transition and animates it into place.
// The nested rAF forces the browser to paint the offset "enter-start" frame
// before the transition is re-enabled — otherwise the style change gets
// batched away and the entrance never animates.
export function useCrossfade<T>(value: T, exitMs = 220) {
  const [display, setDisplay] = useState(value)
  const [phase, setPhase] = useState<CrossfadePhase>('idle')
  const timeoutRef = useRef<ReturnType<typeof setTimeout>>()
  const rafRef = useRef<number>()
  const rafRef2 = useRef<number>()

CrossfadePhase is a union type: a value that can only be one of the listed strings. <T> makes the hook generic, meaning it works for any value type (here a journey object or a screen object) while keeping type safety.

It keeps two pieces of state: display, the value actually on screen (which lags behind the real value), and phase. Three refs hold the ids of the pending timeout and animation frames so the cleanup can cancel them.

Lines 20–41
  useEffect(() => {
    if (value === display) return

    setPhase('exit')
    timeoutRef.current = setTimeout(() => {
      setDisplay(value)
      setPhase('enter-start')
      rafRef.current = requestAnimationFrame(() => {
        rafRef2.current = requestAnimationFrame(() => setPhase('idle'))
      })
    }, exitMs)

    return () => {
      clearTimeout(timeoutRef.current)
      if (rafRef.current) cancelAnimationFrame(rafRef.current)
      if (rafRef2.current) cancelAnimationFrame(rafRef2.current)
    }
    // eslint-disable-next-line react-hooks/exhaustive-deps
  }, [value])

  return { display, phase }
}

When value changes and differs from what is displayed, the phase becomes 'exit'. After exitMs the new value is swapped in with phase 'enter-start', then two nested requestAnimationFrame calls flip it to 'idle'. requestAnimationFrame asks the browser to run a function right before it paints the next frame; nesting two means "after at least one frame has really been painted".

The comparison value === display checks identity, which works because every journey and screen is a fixed object from JOURNEYS. The ESLint comment disables a warning about display not being a dependency; that is on purpose, since the effect should react only to new values, not to its own swap.

src/design-system/composites/landing-page/HowItWorksJourney/HowItWorksJourney.tsx 130 lines

Owns which journey and which screen are active, advances the screen on a timer, handles tab clicks and arrow keys, and runs two independent crossfades: one for the left text and one for the right panel.

Lines 9–21
const SCREEN_DURATION = 1200

export function HowItWorksJourney() {
  const [activeJourneyIndex, setActiveJourneyIndex] = useState(0)
  const [activeScreenIndex, setActiveScreenIndex] = useState(0)

  const tabRefs = useRef<(HTMLButtonElement | null)[]>([])

  const journey = JOURNEYS[activeJourneyIndex]
  const screen = journey.screens[activeScreenIndex]

  const { display: displayJourney, phase: leftPhase } = useCrossfade(journey, 260)
  const { display: displayScreen, phase: screenPhase } = useCrossfade(screen, 240)

Only two numbers are stored in state: the active journey index and the active screen index. The actual objects are looked up from the data on every render. Each is then passed through useCrossfade with its own exit time (260 ms on the left, 240 ms on the right), which returns the value to display and the current phase.

Lines 23–49
  // Auto-advance through the active journey's screens — resets whenever the
  // journey itself changes, since screen count differs per journey (4 for
  // mint, 3 for the rest).
  // Never pauses on hover/focus — it keeps cycling like a marquee.
  useEffect(() => {
    const timer = setInterval(() => {
      setActiveScreenIndex(i => (i + 1) % journey.screens.length)
    }, SCREEN_DURATION)
    return () => clearInterval(timer)
  }, [journey.id, journey.screens.length])

  const handleTabSelect = useCallback((index: number) => {
    setActiveJourneyIndex(index)
    setActiveScreenIndex(0)
  }, [])

  const handleTabKeyDown = useCallback(
    (e: React.KeyboardEvent<HTMLButtonElement>) => {
      if (e.key !== 'ArrowRight' && e.key !== 'ArrowLeft') return
      e.preventDefault()
      const dir = e.key === 'ArrowRight' ? 1 : -1
      const next = (activeJourneyIndex + dir + JOURNEYS.length) % JOURNEYS.length
      handleTabSelect(next)
      tabRefs.current[next]?.focus()
    },
    [activeJourneyIndex, handleTabSelect]
  )

The auto advance is a setInterval that moves to the next screen every SCREEN_DURATION (1200 ms) and wraps with %. As the comment says, it intentionally never pauses on hover or focus; it keeps cycling like a marquee. Its dependencies are the journey id and screen count, so switching journeys clears the old interval and starts a fresh one, giving the first screen of the new journey its full time.

useCallback keeps the same function object between renders unless its dependencies change. handleTabSelect picks a journey and resets to screen 0. handleTabKeyDown implements the standard tabs keyboard pattern: Left and Right arrows move to the previous or next tab, wrapping around, and move keyboard focus to it.

Lines 74–97
      <div className={styles.tabBar} role="tablist" aria-label="Choose a journey">
        {JOURNEYS.map((j, index) => {
          const isActive = index === activeJourneyIndex
          return (
            <button
              key={j.id}
              type="button"
              role="tab"
              id={`journey-tab-${j.id}`}
              aria-selected={isActive}
              aria-controls={`journey-panel-${j.id}`}
              tabIndex={isActive ? 0 : -1}
              ref={el => {
                tabRefs.current[index] = el
              }}
              className={`${styles.tab} ${isActive ? styles.tabActive : ''}`}
              onClick={() => handleTabSelect(index)}
              onKeyDown={handleTabKeyDown}
            >
              {j.tabLabel}
            </button>
          )
        })}
      </div>

These are accessible tabs. role="tablist" and role="tab" tell assistive technology what the buttons are, aria-selected marks the current one and aria-controls links it to the panel. Only the active tab has tabIndex={0}; the others have -1, so the Tab key lands on one tab and the arrows move between them. This is called a roving tabindex. The callback ref stores each button in tabRefs so the arrow handler can focus it.

This is the crossfade in CSS. The base rule is the resting state with a 0.32s transition. [data-phase='exit'] fades the block out while moving it 14px up, with a shorter 0.22s duration. [data-phase='enter-start'] puts it 14px below and invisible with transition: none, so it jumps there instantly. When the phase returns to idle, the base transition animates it up into place.

Lines 428–433
@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) {
  .leftContent,
  .screenVisual {
    transition: none;
  }
}

With reduced motion the two crossfading blocks lose their transitions, so content still swaps (after the same short delay from the hook) but without sliding or fading. The auto advance itself keeps running.

Flow: Clicking a tab

  1. The click. The tab's onClick calls handleTabSelect(index) (HowItWorksJourney.tsx:90), which sets the new journey index and resets the screen index to 0 (HowItWorksJourney.tsx:34 to 37). Arrow keys reach the same function through handleTabKeyDown and also move focus (HowItWorksJourney.tsx:44 to 46).
  2. The rerender. journey and screen now point at the new objects (HowItWorksJourney.tsx:17 to 18). The tab bar updates at once: the new tab gets tabActive, aria-selected and tabIndex={0} (HowItWorksJourney.tsx:83 to 89), and the panel's id updates (HowItWorksJourney.tsx:102).
  3. Both crossfades start. Each useCrossfade sees a value different from what it displays and switches to 'exit' (useCrossfade.ts:21 to 23). The old title and the old screen fade up and out (HowItWorksJourney.module.scss:250, 305).
  4. The timer restarts. Because journey.id changed, the interval effect cleans up the old interval and starts a new one (HowItWorksJourney.tsx:27 to 32), so screen 01 of the new journey stays for a full 1200 ms.
  5. The new content enters. After 240 ms (right) and 260 ms (left) each hook swaps in the new content and plays the entrance described in the next flow (useCrossfade.ts:24 to 29).
  6. Then it carries on. Every 1200 ms the screen index moves on (HowItWorksJourney.tsx:29), only the right panel crossfades because the journey stays the same, and it never pauses when you hover or focus the section.

Flow: The crossfade, frame by frame

  1. A new value arrives. The parent passes a new screen; display still holds the old one, so the old content stays on screen (useCrossfade.ts:14, 21).
  2. Exit. setPhase('exit') (useCrossfade.ts:23) sets data-phase="exit" (JourneyScreenVisual.tsx:15); CSS fades the old content to opacity 0 while moving it 16px up over 0.24s (HowItWorksJourney.module.scss:305 to 309). The hook waits the same 240 ms (HowItWorksJourney.tsx:21).
  3. Enter start. The timeout swaps display to the new value and sets 'enter-start' in the same callback (useCrossfade.ts:24 to 26), so React renders the new content already invisible and 16px low with transition: none (HowItWorksJourney.module.scss:311 to 315). Nothing visibly moves; it jumps there while hidden.
  4. Double requestAnimationFrame. The first frame callback is scheduled, and inside it a second one (useCrossfade.ts:27 to 28). This guarantees the browser has actually painted the hidden low position. Without it, the browser could merge "jump down" and "go back to rest" into one style change and the entrance would not animate.
  5. Idle. setPhase('idle') removes the enter start styles, so the base rule applies again with its 0.35s transition, and the new content slides up and fades in (HowItWorksJourney.module.scss:301 to 303). The same element was reused the whole time; nothing was remounted.
  6. Interruptions are safe. If another value arrives mid fade, the effect's cleanup cancels the pending timeout and both animation frames (useCrossfade.ts:32 to 36) before starting a new exit, so stale swaps never fire.

Roadmap timeline

The roadmap is a horizontal zigzag of five milestones from 2020 to "2027 and Beyond", separated by thin vertical divider lines. React does very little here: it holds two pieces of state (has the timeline been seen, and which card is hovered) and exposes them as data-* attributes. Everything visible, from the staggered drop in entrance to the glowing spark that runs up a divider on hover, is done in CSS keyed off those attributes.

FileLinesRole

src/design-system/composites/landing-page/DomainRoadmap/DomainRoadmap.module.scss376Zigzag layout, divider math, entrance animation, hover spark and tag pop.

Read in this order

  1. DomainRoadmap/index.ts: two lines, just to see how the component is exposed.
  2. DomainRoadmap.tsx: learn the data and the two attributes (data-active, data-spark) the CSS listens for.
  3. DomainRoadmap.module.scss: see what each attribute actually does on screen.

src/design-system/composites/landing-page/DomainRoadmap/DomainRoadmap.tsx 132 lines

Renders the header and an ordered list of milestones. It watches when the list first scrolls into view and tracks which milestone the mouse is over, and passes both facts to CSS as data attributes.

Lines 1–20
import React, { useEffect, useRef, useState } from 'react'

import styles from './DomainRoadmap.module.scss'

interface Milestone {
  id: string
  year: string
  title: string
  description: string
  tags: string[]
}

const MILESTONES: Milestone[] = [
  {
    id: 'idea',
    year: '2020',
    title: 'The Idea',
    description: 'What if your identity on the internet actually belonged to you? We started building before most people understood the problem.',
    tags: ['Founded', 'The Conviction'],
  },

The milestones are plain static data in the file, typed with the Milestone interface. Keeping content in an array means the markup is written once and repeated with .map(). The id field is a stable key for React; an empty tags array means "no pills for this card".

Lines 52–74
export function DomainRoadmap() {
  const timelineRef = useRef<HTMLOListElement>(null)
  // Fires once, the first time the timeline scrolls into view — the cards then stay
  // revealed rather than replaying every time it re-enters the viewport.
  const [active, setActive] = useState(false)
  // Which milestone is currently hovered — drives which divider line gets its
  // traveling spark. null means nothing is hovered.
  const [hoveredIndex, setHoveredIndex] = useState<number | null>(null)

  useEffect(() => {
    if (typeof window === 'undefined' || !timelineRef.current) return
    const observer = new IntersectionObserver(
      entries => {
        if (entries.some(entry => entry.isIntersecting)) {
          setActive(true)
          observer.disconnect()
        }
      },
      { threshold: 0.2 },
    )
    observer.observe(timelineRef.current)
    return () => observer.disconnect()
  }, [])

An IntersectionObserver is a browser API that calls you back when an element enters or leaves the viewport, without you listening to every scroll event. With threshold: 0.2 it fires once 20 percent of the timeline is visible. The callback sets active to true and immediately disconnects, so the reveal plays exactly once and never replays.

The typeof window guard is defensive (effects never run on the server anyway), and the cleanup disconnects if the section unmounts before it was ever seen.

Lines 97–103
      <ol className={styles.timeline} ref={timelineRef} data-active={active || undefined}>
        {/* Nearest divider to a hovered milestone — the last two milestones share the
            rightmost one, since there's one fewer divider than there are milestones. */}
        <span className={styles.dividerLine} data-position="1" data-spark={hoveredIndex === 0 || undefined} aria-hidden="true" />
        <span className={styles.dividerLine} data-position="2" data-spark={hoveredIndex === 1 || undefined} aria-hidden="true" />
        <span className={styles.dividerLine} data-position="3" data-spark={hoveredIndex === 2 || undefined} aria-hidden="true" />
        <span className={styles.dividerLine} data-position="4" data-spark={hoveredIndex === 3 || hoveredIndex === 4 || undefined} aria-hidden="true" />

data-active={active || undefined} is a small trick worth learning. When React sees undefined it leaves the attribute out entirely; when it sees true it writes data-active="true". So the CSS can test for presence with [data-active]. Writing false would not work the same way, because React would render the string "false" and the attribute would still be present.

There are four divider spans for five milestones, so the last two milestones (index 3 and 4) both light the fourth divider. The dividers are aria-hidden because they are purely decorative.

Lines 105–127
        {MILESTONES.map((milestone, index) => (
          <li
            key={milestone.id}
            className={styles.milestone}
            data-offset={index % 2 === 1 || undefined}
            onMouseEnter={() => setHoveredIndex(index)}
            onMouseLeave={() => setHoveredIndex(null)}
          >
            <span className={styles.year}>{milestone.year}</span>
            <h3 className={styles.milestoneTitle}>{milestone.title}</h3>
            <p className={styles.milestoneDescription}>{milestone.description}</p>
            {milestone.tags.length > 0 && (
              <div className={styles.tags}>
                {milestone.tags.map((tag, tagIndex) => (
                  <span className={styles.tag} key={tag} style={{ transitionDelay: `${tagIndex * 60}ms` }}>
                    {tag}
                  </span>
                ))}
              </div>
            )}
          </li>
        ))}
      </ol>

Every odd index (2022 and 2026) gets data-offset, which the CSS pushes down to create the zigzag. Mouse enter and leave update hoveredIndex, which in turn flips data-spark on one divider.

Tags get an inline transitionDelay of 0, 60, 120 milliseconds and so on, so on hover they pop in one after another. Inline styles are the easy way to give each element a value that depends on its position in a list.

src/design-system/composites/landing-page/DomainRoadmap/DomainRoadmap.module.scss 376 lines

Where nearly all the behavior lives. It lays out the zigzag row, positions the dividers mathematically in the gaps, and defines three animations: the entrance, the spark and the tag pop. A prefers-reduced-motion block (lines 273 to 295) turns all of them off for visitors who ask their operating system for less motion.

Lines 110–136
.timeline {
  position: relative;
  display: flex;
  align-items: flex-start;
  justify-content: space-between;
  width: 100%;
  max-width: var(--container-max-width);
  margin: 0;
  padding: 0;
  // Figma's 241px column, shrunk to a fifth of the row (minus 4 gutters) when the row
  // is narrower than 5×241px — small laptops / iPad Pro portrait — so it never overflows.
  --milestone-w: min(241px, calc((100% - 4 * var(--space-4)) / 5));

  // At tablet and below, keep the desktop row layout intact (same cards, same zigzag
  // stagger) instead of stacking into a column — this scrolls horizontally instead.
  @include tablet-down {
    flex-direction: row;
    gap: 0;
    overflow-x: auto;
    -webkit-overflow-scrolling: touch;
    scrollbar-width: none;

    &::-webkit-scrollbar {
      display: none;
    }
  }
}

--milestone-w is a custom property defined locally on the timeline, so only its children can read it. min(241px, ...) keeps the Figma width of 241px but shrinks cards to a fifth of the row (minus four 16px gutters) when the row is too narrow.

Below 992px the layout does not stack into a column; the row simply scrolls sideways with overflow-x: auto, and the scrollbar is hidden in both Firefox (scrollbar-width) and Chromium or Safari (::-webkit-scrollbar).

Lines 147–162
.dividerLine {
  position: absolute;
  top: 0;
  bottom: 0;
  width: 1px;
  background: var(--color-black-alpha-15); // rgba(0,0,0,0.2) ≈ 0.15 Figma value, nearest token
  pointer-events: none;

  // Same gap-center formula, generalised to --milestone-w (equals the old fixed values at 241px).
  &[data-position='1'] { left: calc(1 * var(--milestone-w) + 0.5 * (100% - 5 * var(--milestone-w)) / 4); }
  &[data-position='2'] { left: calc(2 * var(--milestone-w) + 1.5 * (100% - 5 * var(--milestone-w)) / 4); }
  &[data-position='3'] { left: calc(3 * var(--milestone-w) + 2.5 * (100% - 5 * var(--milestone-w)) / 4); }
  &[data-position='4'] { left: calc(4 * var(--milestone-w) + 3.5 * (100% - 5 * var(--milestone-w)) / 4); }

  @include tablet-down { display: none; }
}

Because the cards sit in a space-between row, the gaps between them are not at neat 20, 40, 60, 80 percent positions. Each divider instead uses the exact formula for the center of gap i: i card widths plus (i minus one half) gaps, where one gap is the leftover width divided by four. calc() does this live, so the lines stay centered at any width. They are hidden below 992px, where the row scrolls.

Lines 164–191
// Timeline spark — a small glowing dot travels up the divider line nearest the hovered
// milestone (set via [data-spark] in the component, driven by hover state). Idle state
// just sits invisible at the bottom; the animation only plays while [data-spark] is set,
// so it replays fresh from the bottom on every hover rather than looping continuously.
.dividerLine::after {
  content: '';
  position: absolute;
  left: 50%;
  bottom: 0;
  transform: translateX(-50%);
  width: 6px;
  height: 6px;
  border-radius: var(--radius-full);
  background: var(--color-blue-primary);
  box-shadow: 0 0 8px 2px var(--color-blue-primary-alpha-50);
  opacity: 0;
}

.dividerLine[data-spark]::after {
  animation: divider-spark 0.9s ease-out;
}

@keyframes divider-spark {
  0% { bottom: 0; opacity: 0; }
  15% { opacity: 1; }
  85% { opacity: 1; }
  100% { bottom: 100%; opacity: 0; }
}

The spark is a ::after pseudo element (an extra box CSS can draw inside an element without any markup) shaped as a 6px glowing dot. It normally sits invisible at the bottom. Only while the divider has data-spark does the divider-spark keyframe animation play, moving bottom from 0 to 100 percent and fading in and out.

Because the animation is attached only while the attribute is present, removing the attribute and adding it back restarts it from the bottom. The animation runs once (no infinite repeat), so a long hover shows one spark.

Lines 228–256
.milestone {
  opacity: 0;
  transform: translateY(-56px);
  transition: opacity 0.6s ease, transform 0.7s cubic-bezier(0.22, 0.61, 0.36, 1);

  &[data-offset] {
    transform: translateY(56px);
  }

  // Row layout all the way down from tablet through mobile, so the zigzag entrance
  // direction (top row falls from above, offset row rises from below) applies at
  // both widths instead of collapsing to a single stacked-column direction.
  @include tablet-down {
    transform: translateY(-56px);

    &[data-offset] {
      transform: translateY(56px);
    }
  }
}

.timeline[data-active] .milestone {
  opacity: 1;
  transform: translateY(0);

  &[data-offset] {
    transform: translateY(0);
  }
}

The entrance is a CSS transition, not a keyframe animation. Before activation, top row cards sit 56px above their spot and offset cards 56px below, all at zero opacity. When .timeline[data-active] matches, the target values become opacity 1 and no offset, and the transition line animates between them. The cubic-bezier curve makes the motion decelerate softly as it lands.

Lines 258–271
// Focus dimming — hovering one milestone fades the other four back, narrowing
// attention to the one being read. Overrides the base active opacity above.
.timeline[data-active] .milestone[data-dimmed] {
  opacity: 0.45;
}

// Staggered by DOM position among the <li> siblings only — :nth-of-type ignores the
// four <span> dividers that precede them, so this stays correct without needing an
// inline per-item delay from the component.
.milestone:nth-of-type(1) { transition-delay: 0s; }
.milestone:nth-of-type(2) { transition-delay: 0.15s; }
.milestone:nth-of-type(3) { transition-delay: 0.3s; }
.milestone:nth-of-type(4) { transition-delay: 0.45s; }
.milestone:nth-of-type(5) { transition-delay: 0.6s; }

Each card waits a little longer than the previous one, so they appear left to right. :nth-of-type counts only siblings of the same tag, so the four <span> dividers placed before the <li> items do not throw the count off.

The [data-dimmed] rule is currently unused: the component never sets that attribute, so hovering does not dim the other cards.

Lines 319–376
// Year and title glow — the hovered card's year and title lift slightly and pick up a
// soft blue glow, so the hovered era reads as coming forward off the timeline.
.milestone:hover .year,
.milestone:hover .milestoneTitle {
  transform: translateY(-4px);
  filter: drop-shadow(0 4px 10px var(--color-blue-primary-alpha-30));
}
// …
  // Tag pop — idles slightly scaled down and dimmed, then switches fully on with a
  // staggered delay (set inline per-tag in the component) when its card is hovered.
  transform: scale(0.9);
  opacity: 0.8;
  transition: transform 0.35s cubic-bezier(0.34, 1.4, 0.64, 1), opacity 0.3s ease;
// …
.milestone:hover .tag {
  transform: scale(1);
  opacity: 1;
}

The hover effects are plain CSS :hover, independent of React state. The year and title lift 4px and gain a blue drop-shadow (unlike box-shadow, it follows the shape of the gradient text). Tags idle at 90 percent size and 80 percent opacity and spring to full size; the overshooting cubic-bezier(0.34, 1.4, ...) gives the little bounce, and the inline delays from the component stagger them.

Flow: The timeline reveal and the hover spark

  1. Initial render. The server HTML has no data-active on the list, so DomainRoadmap.module.scss:228 keeps all five cards transparent and shifted up or down.
  2. Observer starts. After hydration the effect at DomainRoadmap.tsx:61 creates an IntersectionObserver and watches the <ol>.
  3. Timeline enters the viewport. Once 20 percent is visible the callback runs setActive(true) and disconnects (DomainRoadmap.tsx:65), so it never fires again.
  4. Attribute appears. React rerenders and writes data-active="true" on the list (DomainRoadmap.tsx:97).
  5. Cards transition in. .timeline[data-active] .milestone (DomainRoadmap.module.scss:249) now matches, and each card fades and slides into place with the delays from DomainRoadmap.module.scss:267 onward: 0s, 0.15s, 0.3s, 0.45s, 0.6s.
  6. Mouse enters a card. onMouseEnter sets hoveredIndex (DomainRoadmap.tsx:110). At the same moment CSS :hover lifts the year and title and pops the tags.
  7. Spark plays. The matching divider gets data-spark (DomainRoadmap.tsx:100), which attaches the 0.9 second divider-spark animation (DomainRoadmap.module.scss:182), and the dot travels from bottom to top.
  8. Mouse leaves. hoveredIndex goes back to null, the attribute is removed, and the next hover restarts the spark from the bottom. Moving directly between the last two cards keeps the fourth divider's attribute in place, so the spark does not replay there.

Payments and partners marquees

This section has two halves, "Pay Your Way" and "Strong Partners", each with a header and an endless row of logo cards sliding to the left. The sliding is not a CSS animation: a callback registered on GSAP's ticker moves each row a few pixels every frame and quietly wraps it back by exactly one copy of the logos, so the loop has no visible seam. The headers fade up with GSAP ScrollTrigger when they scroll into view.

GSAP is an animation library; ScrollTrigger is its plugin for starting animations based on scroll position. Plugins must be registered once. The typeof window check skips registration during server rendering, where there is no browser to scroll.

An endless marquee needs the row to be wider than the screen even after it has moved one full copy to the left. So the eight partner logos are spread three times (24 cards), and the payment logos six times. The comment says "only 4 logos" but the array actually holds five, giving 30 cards. That still works, because the effect divides by the repetition count rather than assuming a number.

LogoCard is a tiny component; every card has a fixed width from CSS, so measuring one copy is reliable even before the images finish loading.

Lines 57–73
const IdentityOsStatsAndPartners: React.FC = () => {
  // GSAP refs
  const payRow1Ref = useRef<HTMLDivElement>(null)
  const partRow1Ref = useRef<HTMLDivElement>(null)
  const paymentsHeaderRef = useRef<HTMLDivElement>(null)
  const partnersHeaderRef = useRef<HTMLDivElement>(null)

  useEffect(() => {
    // Seamless marquee via gsap.ticker — x accumulates forever and wraps mod oneSet,
    // so there is never a reset jump. Content is 3× repeated; oneSet = scrollWidth / 3.
    const tickerCallbacks: (() => void)[] = []
    const marqueeCtx = {
      revert: () => {
        tickerCallbacks.forEach(cb => gsap.ticker.remove(cb))
        tickerCallbacks.length = 0
      },
    }

Four refs point at the two moving rows and the two header wrappers. marqueeCtx is a hand made object with a revert method, mirroring the shape of a real gsap.context, so the cleanup at the end can treat both the same way. Its job is to remove every ticker callback this effect added.

Lines 74–98
    ;[
      { el: payRow1Ref.current, speed: 80, dir: -1, reps: 6 },
      { el: partRow1Ref.current, speed: 38, dir: -1, reps: 3 },
    ].forEach(({ el, speed, dir, reps }) => {
      if (!el) return
      // Measure one set using getBoundingClientRect so margins between cards are included.
      const children = Array.from(el.children) as HTMLElement[]
      const first = children[0].getBoundingClientRect()
      const last = children[children.length / reps - 1].getBoundingClientRect()
      const oneSet = last.right - first.left
      let x = dir === 1 ? -oneSet : 0
      gsap.set(el, { x })
      let lastTime = gsap.ticker.time
      const cb = () => {
        const now = gsap.ticker.time
        const dt = now - lastTime
        lastTime = now
        x += dir * speed * dt
        if (dir === -1 && x <= -oneSet) x += oneSet
        if (dir === 1 && x >= 0) x -= oneSet
        gsap.set(el, { x })
      }
      gsap.ticker.add(cb)
      tickerCallbacks.push(cb)
    })

The ticker is GSAP's single requestAnimationFrame loop; anything added to it runs once per screen refresh. For each row the code measures the width of one copy (left edge of the first card to the right edge of the last card of the first copy), then on every frame moves x by speed times elapsed time and wraps it.

Using elapsed time (dt, in seconds) instead of a fixed step per frame keeps the speed at 80 or 38 pixels per second on both 60Hz and 120Hz screens. gsap.set applies the value instantly as a transform: translate, which the browser can move cheaply without recalculating layout. The dir === 1 branches support right moving rows, though both rows here move left.

Lines 100–126
    const fadeCtx = gsap.context(() => {
      ;[paymentsHeaderRef, partnersHeaderRef].forEach(ref => {
        if (!ref.current) return
        gsap.fromTo(
          ref.current.children,
          { opacity: 0, y: 48 },
          {
            opacity: 1,
            y: 0,
            duration: 0.75,
            ease: 'power2.out',
            stagger: 0.12,
            scrollTrigger: {
              trigger: ref.current,
              start: 'top 82%',
              toggleActions: 'play none none none',
            },
          }
        )
      })
    })

    return () => {
      marqueeCtx.revert()
      fadeCtx.revert()
    }
  }, [])

gsap.context() records every animation and ScrollTrigger created inside its callback, so fadeCtx.revert() can undo them all at once. Each header's children start 48px lower and invisible, then rise into place over 0.75 seconds, 0.12 seconds apart, once the header's top reaches 82 percent down the viewport. toggleActions: 'play none none none' means it plays once and does nothing when you scroll back.

The returned cleanup runs on unmount (and twice in development Strict Mode), which stops ticker callbacks from piling up.

Every card is exactly 250px wide (200px under 992px) and will not shrink, which is what makes the width of one copy predictable. One caveat: the copy width is measured once when the effect runs, so if the window is resized across the 992px line afterwards, the wrap distance no longer matches the card size and the loop can jump once per cycle until the page reloads.

Flow: One frame of the gsap.ticker marquee

  1. Setup, once. On mount the effect reads the row's children (IdentityOsStatsAndPartners.tsx:80). For the partner row that is 24 cards with reps: 3, so the last card of the first copy is index 24 / 3 minus 1, which is 7.
  2. Measure one set. oneSet = last.right - first.left (IdentityOsStatsAndPartners.tsx:83). With eight 250px cards that is 2000px. getBoundingClientRect is used so any spacing between cards would be included too.
  3. Start position. Moving left, x starts at 0 and is applied with gsap.set (IdentityOsStatsAndPartners.tsx:84). lastTime remembers the ticker's clock.
  4. A frame arrives. The browser is about to paint, GSAP's ticker fires, and the callback reads gsap.ticker.time. If 1/60 of a second passed, dt is about 0.0167 (IdentityOsStatsAndPartners.tsx:89).
  5. Advance. x += dir * speed * dt: for the partners row that is minus 38 times 0.0167, about 0.63px to the left this frame (IdentityOsStatsAndPartners.tsx:91).
  6. Wrap. When x reaches minus 2000, one whole copy has scrolled past and the second copy sits exactly where the first one started. Adding oneSet puts x back near 0 (IdentityOsStatsAndPartners.tsx:92). The pixels on screen are identical before and after, so the jump is invisible; and because the leftover fraction is kept, the speed never stutters.
  7. Apply. gsap.set(el, { x }) writes the new transform (IdentityOsStatsAndPartners.tsx:94) and the frame is painted. This repeats every frame until unmount, when marqueeCtx.revert() removes the callback (IdentityOsStatsAndPartners.tsx:123).

Blog teaser slider

The blog teaser shows up to six recent posts as cards in a horizontally scrolling row, with previous and next arrows and a small progress bar. The posts arrive as a prop from getStaticProps, so no fetching happens in the browser. The row is a native scroll container with CSS scroll snapping; the arrows simply ask it to scroll by one card width, and the component reads the scroll position back to enable or disable the arrows and fill the progress bar.
