src/design-system/composites/landing-page/DomainBlogTeaser/DomainBlogTeaser.tsx 129 lines

Receives the posts, renders the header, the arrow controls and one BlogCard per post, and keeps three pieces of state in sync with the row's scroll position.

Lines 8–15
const FEATURED_COUNT = 6

interface DomainBlogTeaserProps {
  posts?: BlogSummary[]
}

export function DomainBlogTeaser({ posts: postsProp = [] }: DomainBlogTeaserProps) {
  const posts = postsProp.slice(0, FEATURED_COUNT)

The prop is optional and defaults to an empty array (posts: postsProp = [] renames it and sets a default in one go). slice(0, 6) caps the count; since getFeaturedPosts asks Ghost for five, in practice the row shows at most five.

Lines 20–31
  const rowRef = useRef<HTMLDivElement | null>(null)
  const roRef = useRef<ResizeObserver | null>(null)
  const [canPrev, setCanPrev] = useState(false)
  const [canNext, setCanNext] = useState(false)
  const [scrollProgress, setScrollProgress] = useState(0)

  const updateArrows = useCallback((el: HTMLDivElement) => {
    setCanPrev(el.scrollLeft > 4)
    setCanNext(el.scrollLeft < el.scrollWidth - el.clientWidth - 4)
    const max = el.scrollWidth - el.clientWidth
    setScrollProgress(max > 0 ? el.scrollLeft / max : 0)
  }, [])

updateArrows is the single place that reads the row's scroll geometry. scrollLeft is how far it has scrolled, scrollWidth is the full content width and clientWidth the visible width, so scrollWidth - clientWidth is the maximum scroll. The 4px tolerance avoids flicker from fractional pixels. Progress is a number from 0 to 1. useCallback keeps the same function between renders, so other callbacks that depend on it stay stable too.

Lines 33–51
  const setRowRef = useCallback(
    (el: HTMLDivElement | null) => {
      if (roRef.current) {
        roRef.current.disconnect()
        roRef.current = null
      }
      rowRef.current = el
      if (!el) return
      requestAnimationFrame(() => updateArrows(el))
      const ro = new ResizeObserver(() => requestAnimationFrame(() => updateArrows(el)))
      ro.observe(el)
      roRef.current = ro
    },
    [updateArrows]
  )

  const handleScroll = useCallback(() => {
    if (rowRef.current) updateArrows(rowRef.current)
  }, [updateArrows])

setRowRef is a callback ref: instead of an object, React calls this function with the DOM element when it mounts (and with null when it unmounts). That gives a natural place to attach a ResizeObserver, a browser API that reports when an element changes size, so the arrows update when the window is resized. requestAnimationFrame defers the measurement to just before the next paint, when layout is final. Any previous observer is disconnected first so none are leaked.

handleScroll reads the geometry again on every scroll event, covering touch swipes and trackpads as well as the arrows.

Lines 53–66
  const slide = useCallback(
    (dir: 'prev' | 'next') => {
      const el = rowRef.current
      if (!el) return
      const card = el.querySelector('[role="listitem"]') as HTMLElement | null
      const cardWidth = card ? card.getBoundingClientRect().width : el.clientWidth / 3
      const target = el.scrollLeft + (dir === 'next' ? cardWidth : -cardWidth)
      el.scrollTo({ left: target, behavior: 'smooth' })
      setTimeout(() => updateArrows(el), 400)
    },
    [updateArrows]
  )

  if (!posts.length) return null

slide measures the first card (the element with role="listitem"), or falls back to a third of the row, and asks the browser to scroll smoothly by that amount. The 400ms timeout is a safety net that refreshes the arrows once the smooth scroll has probably finished.

Line 66 returns null when there are no posts, which removes the whole section. It sits after the hooks on purpose: hooks must run in the same order on every render, so an early return is only allowed below them.

Lines 92–116
      <div className={styles.controlsRow}>
        <div className={styles.sliderBtns}>
          <button
            type="button"
            className={styles.sliderBtn}
            onClick={() => slide('prev')}
            disabled={!canPrev}
            aria-label="Previous posts"
          >
            <FiChevronLeft size={18} aria-hidden="true" />
          </button>

            <div className={styles.sliderProgressFill} style={{ width: `${scrollProgress * 100}%` }} />

          <button
            type="button"

          </button>
        </div>
      </div>

Real <button> elements with type="button", an aria-label each, and disabled tied to state, so keyboard and screen reader users get correct behavior for free. The progress fill's width is set inline from scrollProgress; CSS animates the change.

src/design-system/composites/landing-page/DomainBlogTeaser/BlogCard.tsx 122 lines

One clickable card linking to /blog/slug. It formats views and date, shows image, category, reading time, title, excerpt and author, and runs a small tooltip that follows the cursor.

Lines 14–32
export function BlogCard({ post, basePath = '/blog' }: BlogCardProps) {
  const author = post.author
  const views = post.views >= 1000 ? `${(post.views / 1000).toFixed(1)}k` : String(post.views)

  const [hovered, setHovered] = useState(false)
  const [pos, setPos] = useState({ x: 0, y: 0 })

  const handleMouseMove = useCallback((e: React.MouseEvent<HTMLAnchorElement>) => {
    const rect = e.currentTarget.getBoundingClientRect()
    setPos({ x: e.clientX - rect.left, y: e.clientY - rect.top })
  }, [])

  const handleMouseEnter = useCallback((e: React.MouseEvent<HTMLAnchorElement>) => {
    const rect = e.currentTarget.getBoundingClientRect()
    setPos({ x: e.clientX - rect.left, y: e.clientY - rect.top })
    setHovered(true)
  }, [])

  const handleMouseLeave = useCallback(() => setHovered(false), [])

Views above 999 become "1.2k". The mouse handlers store the cursor position relative to the card's own top left corner (clientX minus the card's left edge), which is what the tooltip needs. currentTarget is the element the handler is attached to, the link, even if the mouse is over a child.

Lines 34–48
  return (
    <Link
      href={`${basePath}/${post.slug}`}
      className={styles.card}
      aria-label={post.title}
      onMouseEnter={handleMouseEnter}
      onMouseMove={handleMouseMove}
      onMouseLeave={handleMouseLeave}
    >
      {/* Tooltip — follows cursor */}
      <span
        className={`${styles.tooltip} ${hovered ? styles.tooltipVisible : ''}`}
        style={{ '--tx': `${pos.x}px`, '--ty': `${pos.y}px` } as React.CSSProperties}
        aria-hidden="true"
      >Read whole blog</span>

next/link renders an <a> and makes navigation client side. The tooltip position is passed as two CSS custom properties, --tx and --ty, set inline; the stylesheet reads them with var(). The cast to React.CSSProperties is needed because TypeScript does not know about custom property names.

}

Scroll snapping makes a scroll container come to rest on defined points. scroll-snap-type: x mandatory on the row plus scroll-snap-align: start on each card means any scroll, by finger or by the arrows, settles with a card's left edge aligned to the row's start. That is why the arrow can scroll by just the card width even though the 20px gap is not included: snapping corrects the final position.

Cards are 434px wide, or 85 percent of the row on small screens so the next card peeks in. The divider before every card except the first is centered in the gap with calc(-1 * var(--space-5) / 2 - 0.5px).

}

clip-path: polygon(...) cuts an element to a shape given as a list of corner points. Here the image keeps only six points, which removes a 20px triangle at the top left and at the bottom right. aspect-ratio: 16 / 9 reserves the image height before it loads, so the layout does not jump.

The title is clamped to three lines with the -webkit-line-clamp trio, which all modern browsers support. On card hover it switches to gradient text: the gradient is painted as a background, background-clip: text limits it to the letter shapes, and a transparent text fill lets it show through.

Flow: Clicking the next arrow

  1. Initial state. After mount, the callback ref schedules updateArrows (DomainBlogTeaser.tsx:41). At scroll 0, canPrev is false and canNext is true if the cards overflow, so only the next button is enabled.
  2. Click. The button calls slide('next') (DomainBlogTeaser.tsx:109).
  3. Measure a card. slide finds the first [role="listitem"] and reads its width, 434px on desktop (DomainBlogTeaser.tsx:57).
  4. Scroll smoothly. The target is the current scrollLeft plus that width, passed to el.scrollTo with behavior: 'smooth' (DomainBlogTeaser.tsx:60). The browser animates the scroll itself.
  5. Snap. Because of scroll-snap-type: x mandatory (DomainBlogTeaser.module.scss:201), the row settles with the second card aligned to the start, covering the 20px gap the measurement left out.
  6. State follows. Each scroll event calls handleScroll then updateArrows (DomainBlogTeaser.tsx:49): canPrev turns true, scrollProgress grows, and the fill width transitions. The 400ms timeout (DomainBlogTeaser.tsx:61) runs one final update.
  7. At the end. When scrollLeft comes within 4px of the maximum, canNext becomes false and the next button disables itself (DomainBlogTeaser.tsx:28).

Frequently asked questions

The FAQ closes the page: a left column with the heading, and a right column accordion of seven hard coded questions, of which four show until "View More" is pressed. Only one answer is open at a time. Opening and closing are animated with GSAP tweening the answer's height between 0 and its natural height, and on desktop the left column stays pinned with position: sticky while the longer list scrolls past.

  {

    isDeleted: false,
    position: 0,
    question: 'What exactly am I buying?',
    answer:
      "A domain that is your identity: a permanent, on-chain name minted to your wallet. Unlike Web2 handles you only rent, no platform can revoke, suspend, or reassign it. You hold the keys, so you own the identity. Forever.",
  },
  {

The questions used to come from an API (see the legacy file); now they are a static array typed with the existing FaqsType, which is why fields like isDeleted and position are still filled in even though nothing reads them. Lines 70–90
interface FaqItemProps {
  faq: FaqsType
  isOpen: boolean
  onToggle: () => void
  isFirst: boolean
}

const FaqItem = ({ faq, isOpen, onToggle, isFirst }: FaqItemProps) => {
  const answerRef = useRef<HTMLDivElement>(null)
  const prevOpen = useRef<boolean>(isOpen)

  useEffect(() => {
    const el = answerRef.current
    if (!el) return

    // Skip animation on first mount — just set the initial state
    if (prevOpen.current === isOpen && el.dataset.initialized !== '1') {
      gsap.set(el, { height: isOpen ? 'auto' : 0, opacity: isOpen ? 1 : 0 })
      el.dataset.initialized = '1'
      return
    }

Each item keeps two refs: the answer element, and prevOpen, the open state from the previous run, which survives rerenders without causing one. On the very first run the item should not animate, so it uses gsap.set (an instant jump) to height auto if open or 0 if closed, and marks the element with data-initialized="1".

The dataset flag matters in development Strict Mode, where React runs effects twice: the second run sees the flag and does not mistake itself for the first mount.

Lines 92–120
    prevOpen.current = isOpen

    if (isOpen) {
      gsap.set(el, { height: 0, opacity: 0 })
      gsap.to(el, {
        height: 'auto',
        duration: 0.4,
        ease: 'power2.out',
      })
      gsap.to(el, {
        opacity: 1,
        duration: 0.3,
        delay: 0.1,
        ease: 'power1.out',
      })
    } else {
      gsap.to(el, {
        opacity: 0,
        duration: 0.2,
        ease: 'power1.in',
      })
      gsap.to(el, {
        height: 0,
        duration: 0.35,
        delay: 0.05,
        ease: 'power2.inOut',
      })
    }
  }, [isOpen])

A tween is an animation of one or more properties from a start value to an end value over time. CSS cannot transition to height: auto, but GSAP can: it measures the natural height and animates to it. Opening grows the height over 0.4s and fades the text in slightly later, so text never appears in a half open box. Closing does the reverse order: fade out first, then shrink. The two tweens of each direction run at the same time with different durations and delays.

Lines 122–140
  return (
    <div className={`${styles.faqItem} ${isOpen ? styles.faqItemOpen : ''} ${isFirst ? styles.faqItemFirst : ''}`}>
      <button className={styles.faqRow} onClick={onToggle} aria-expanded={isOpen} aria-controls={`faq-answer-${faq.id}`}>
        <span className={styles.faqQuestion}>{faq.question}</span>
        <IoIosArrowDown size={24} className={styles.caretIcon} aria-hidden="true" />

The question is a real <button>, so it works with the keyboard. aria-expanded announces whether it is open, and aria-controls points at the answer's id; the answer is a labelled region. The open item gets faqItemOpen, which CSS uses for the gradient background, white text and the rotated caret.

Lines 142–148
const FAQ_INITIAL_COUNT = 4

const FrequentlyAskedQuestions = () => {
  const [openId, setOpenId] = useState<string | null>(FAQS[0]?.id ?? null)
  const [showAll, setShowAll] = useState(false)

  const toggle = (id: string) => setOpenId(prev => (prev === id ? null : id))

The parent stores only the id of the open item, which guarantees that at most one is open. It starts with the first question open. toggle uses the functional form of the setter (prev => ...) so it always compares against the latest value: clicking the open item closes it, clicking another switches.

Lines 175–196
        {/* Right: accordion */}
        <div className={styles.right}>
          {(showAll ? FAQS : FAQS.slice(0, FAQ_INITIAL_COUNT)).map((faq, index) =>
            React.createElement(
              React.Fragment,
              { key: String(faq.id) },
              index > 0 ? <div className={styles.divider} aria-hidden="true" /> : null,
              <FaqItem faq={faq} isOpen={openId === faq.id} onToggle={() => toggle(faq.id)} isFirst={index === 0} />,
            )
          )}
          {FAQS.length > FAQ_INITIAL_COUNT && (
            <div>
              <button
                className={styles.viewMoreBtn}
                onClick={() => setShowAll(prev => !prev)}
              >
                {showAll ? 'View Less' : 'View More'}
                <IoIosArrowDown className={showAll ? styles.viewMoreArrowOpen : styles.viewMoreArrow} />
              </button>
            </div>
          )}
        </div>

The list shows four or all seven items. Each entry is a Fragment holding a divider (skipped before the first) and the item; React.createElement(React.Fragment, { key }, ...) is the long form of <React.Fragment key=...>. When "View More" adds items 5 to 7, they mount fresh, closed, and run the first mount branch of their effect.

src/design-system/composites/main-page/banner-faq/FrequentlyAskedQuestions.module.scss 247 lines

Imports the mixins through the @newstyles alias (the landing sections use a relative path instead; both resolve to the same file). It sets up the two columns, the sticky behavior and every visual state of an item.

Lines 4–13
.section {
  background: var(--color-white-primary);
  position: relative;
  padding: var(--section-spacing, 80px) 0;
  // clip (not hidden) so .left's position: sticky still sticks to the viewport —
  // overflow: hidden would make this section the sticky scroll container.
  overflow: clip;
  border-top: 0.5px solid var(--color-border-black-subtle);
  border-bottom: 0.5px solid var(--color-border-black-subtle);
}

overflow: clip cuts off overflowing content like hidden does, but unlike hidden it does not turn the section into a scroll container. That difference is the whole reason sticky positioning still works inside it, the same lesson as pages/index.module.scss.

From 1025px up (the desktop mixin) the left column is 450px wide and position: sticky; top: 100px: it scrolls normally until its top is 100px from the viewport top, then stays there until the bottom of its parent container pushes it away. Below that width the columns stack and nothing sticks.

  color: var(--color-white-primary);
  transform: rotate(180deg);
}

In the open state the question turns white and medium weight, and the caret rotates 180 degrees with a CSS transition. These are the parts of the open animation that CSS handles, while GSAP handles the answer.

Lines 191–197
// ── Answer panel (height + opacity driven by GSAP) ───────────────────────────
.answerInner {
  padding: 0 var(--space-5) var(--space-7) var(--space-4);
  overflow: hidden;
  height: 0;
  opacity: 0;
}

Every answer starts collapsed in CSS: height 0, invisible, and overflow: hidden so text does not spill out while the height animates. Because effects only run after hydration, the first question also appears closed in the server HTML and pops open, without animation, the moment its effect runs gsap.set.

For comparison: this version fetched questions in the browser after mount (so they were missing from server HTML), tracked the open item by array position plus one, and calls getFaqs() from two identical effects, so it would have fetched twice. The new component replaces all of that with static data and an id based open state.

Flow: Opening a question

  1. Starting point. openId holds the first question's id (FrequentlyAskedQuestions.tsx:145), and its effect has already set its answer to height auto on mount (FrequentlyAskedQuestions.tsx:87).
  2. Click. The visitor clicks the third question's button, which calls onToggle, then toggle(id) (FrequentlyAskedQuestions.tsx:148). The new id differs from the previous one, so openId becomes the third id.
  3. Rerender. The parent rerenders; the first item now receives isOpen={false} and the third isOpen={true} (FrequentlyAskedQuestions.tsx:182). Each gets or loses faqItemOpen, so CSS immediately switches backgrounds, text color and caret rotation (FrequentlyAskedQuestions.module.scss:141, :175, :186).
  4. Effects fire. Both items' effects run because isOpen changed (FrequentlyAskedQuestions.tsx:120). Both are already initialized, so they skip the first mount branch and update prevOpen.
  5. Close tween. The first item fades its answer out in 0.2s and shrinks it to 0 over 0.35s (FrequentlyAskedQuestions.tsx:108).
  6. Open tween. At the same time the third item resets to 0, then GSAP measures its natural height and grows to it in 0.4s, fading text in from 0.1s (FrequentlyAskedQuestions.tsx:95). Because one shrinks while the other grows, the list height changes smoothly.
  7. Sticky column. On a desktop screen, as the visitor scrolls the now taller list, .left stays 100px below the top (FrequentlyAskedQuestions.module.scss:48), which works because the section uses overflow: clip (FrequentlyAskedQuestions.module.scss:10) and the page wrapper avoids overflow: hidden (pages/index.module.scss:1).
